# Changelog

All notable changes to Excalibur and Excalibur.Dispatch are documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

> **How to read this file.** `10.0.0` has not been released. `[Unreleased]` collects what has landed
> since the `10.0.0-alpha.8` pre-release; `[10.0.0-alpha.8]` records what shipped in that pre-release.
> Earlier `10.0.0-alpha.*` pre-releases are not documented individually — if you are on one of those,
> move to the newest and read both sections in full; that is the upgrade path we support.
>
> `[3.0.0-alpha]` is the previous line, summarised cumulatively for the same reason: nothing in it
> shipped as a separately-documented release. Moving from `3.0.0-alpha` to `10.0.0` is one step,
> not a sequence.

## [Unreleased]

Changes landed since `10.0.0-alpha.8`. Breaking changes are marked **BREAKING** and are grouped at the
top of each heading; this line is pre-RC, so they are frequent and are the first thing to read.
Everything requiring action from you is also collected under **Before you upgrade** on the
[What's New](https://docs.excalibur-dispatch.dev/docs/whats-new) page.

### Known Issues

Defects identified and classified as affecting this pre-release. The full descriptions, with the action
each one requires of you, are on the **Known issues in this pre-release** section of the What's New page;
they are kept there rather than duplicated here so the two cannot drift apart. This list reflects what we
have classified, not everything that exists — see the What's New page for the limits of that claim.

- **A database provisioned by an earlier pre-release keeps a wider tenant column, and re-running the create script will not narrow it.** The tenant identifier column is now declared 64 characters wide across every provider that stores one. The shipped create scripts skip any table that already exists, so an upgraded database keeps whatever width it was created with while a fresh install gets 64. Nothing breaks at runtime: collation and nullability are unchanged, and reads and writes behave identically on both shapes. It matters where the two shapes meet -- a restore, a replica, or a move to another provider -- because two tenant identifiers differing only after the 64th character are distinct in the wider column and identical in the narrower one, and the tenant identifier is part of some primary keys. You are unaffected in practice unless you set tenant identifiers longer than 64 characters before the limit existed. **A narrowing script now ships for each affected store, so this is something you can act on.** Run the one for your provider against an upgraded database; it narrows the column in place. It refuses and changes nothing if any stored tenant identifier is longer than 64 characters, rather than truncating a value that identifies a tenant -- so shorten or re-key those tenants first, then re-run it. Running one against a database that is already narrow does nothing. Affected stores: outbox, inbox, saga, snapshot, audit and dead-letter on SQL Server, Oracle and PostgreSQL, depending on provider. The PostgreSQL inbox is the sharpest case: its tenant column was previously unbounded rather than merely wider, and the tenant is the third component of that table's deduplication key -- so on an upgraded database two tenants whose identifiers agree in their first 64 characters are distinct today and would share one deduplication scope if the column were narrowed by truncation, silently suppressing one tenant's message as a duplicate of the other's. Its script refuses for that reason.
- **Cache tag invalidation is exact only on Redis.** On any other distributed cache backend the tag-to-keys set is maintained with a read-modify-write, because the abstraction offers no atomic set-add. Two instances registering different keys under one tag concurrently can lose one of them, and a key dropped from the set is not invalidated when its tag is, so that entry serves stale data until its own expiry. The framework now logs a warning at startup when it selects this tracker, and the caching documentation says so. Register a Redis connection if you depend on tag invalidation being exact, or keep entry lifetimes short enough that the staleness window is acceptable.
- **Packages published at `10.0.0-alpha.8` declare a benchmarking harness as a direct dependency.** 103 of the 195 packages published at that version bring `BenchmarkDotNet` and three compiler-platform packages into your restore graph. Nothing calls into them at runtime, so this changes what you restore and audit rather than what you run. Move to a later pre-release; there is no workaround while you remain on that version.
- **The bundled Cosmos DB emulator fixture cannot connect using its documented approach.** Its documentation blames the emulator's self-signed certificate; the actual obstacle is that the client is sent to the emulator's advertised port rather than the mapped one. Set `CosmosClientOptions.LimitToEndpoint = true`.
- **Integration coverage has known failures.** Cosmos DB integration tests now run nightly rather than being excluded outright, but **we have not yet published a build in which they executed and passed.** Until we do, treat the Cosmos DB provider as materially less proven than the others and validate the operations you depend on against your own infrastructure. When those tests are run manually, some do not pass, and we have not resolved those failures. A recent full run also showed failures outside Cosmos DB, but almost all were test containers failing to start on the machine running the suite — a local resource limit rather than provider defects, and we are not reporting them as such.
- **Unexplained not-found responses from the Cosmos DB snapshot store.** Cause not determined, and we have not established whether it originates in the provider or in our own test setup. If you see one where data should be present, do not treat it as authoritative, and please report it.

### Changed

#### Error handling and the dispatch contract

- **BREAKING — an exception thrown by a message handler now reaches you instead of being converted to a generic failed result, and a permanently-failing handler is no longer retried.** The final dispatch stage used to catch every handler fault and return a `500` problem typed `urn:dispatch:error:handler-error`, so a registered `IExceptionMapper`, an `ITypedExceptionHandler`, and your own `catch` never saw it — and the behaviour depended on whether any middleware was registered. The exception now propagates on every path, exactly as `IActionHandler<TAction>` has always documented. Because the old generic `500` classified as a transient server error, retry re-ran handlers for faults that could never succeed (an `ArgumentException` ran three times under the default attempt count, taking a non-idempotent handler's side effects once per attempt); retry now classifies the handler's own exception and abandons permanent faults after one attempt. **If you branch on `IsSuccess` after dispatching an action whose handler throws, you must now catch the exception**, map it with `UseExceptionMapping()`, or handle it with `UseTypedExceptionHandling()`. A failure a handler *returns* as a result is unaffected, as is a transport-bus delivery failure. One asymmetry: a fan-out publish to several buses still yields a failed result naming the failed legs rather than an exception, so no mapper runs for it — read the problem details instead.
- **BREAKING — the retry middleware and the circuit breaker no longer convert a fault into a failed result.** Both used to return a problem of their own (`RetryError`, `RetryExhausted`, `CircuitBreakerFailure`), destroying whatever your handler threw before anything above them ran. Retry decides how many attempts a fault gets and the breaker decides whether to keep letting faults through; neither decides what the fault is. The original exception, with its type and message intact, is now raised after one attempt for a permanent fault and after the configured attempts for a transient one, and the breaker records the failure and lets the exception through. **If you branch on `IsSuccess` after `UseRetry()` or `UseCircuitBreaker()`, you must now catch the exception** or map it.
- **BREAKING — dispatching an action with no registered handler now throws instead of returning a failed result.** A missing registration is a configuration fault, not a request outcome. It previously arrived as `IsSuccess == false` with the message *"No handler registered for action OrphanAction"*, so following our own documented `return BadRequest(result.ErrorMessage)` pattern turned a forgotten registration into an **HTTP 400 blaming the end user's request**, and it was found in production rather than development.
- **BREAKING — inline projection failures throw `AggregateException`; `InlineProjectionException` is removed.** With the `Propagate` policy a failed projection has always raised an `AggregateException` carrying one inner exception per failure. `InlineProjectionException` was declared and documented but never thrown, so a `catch` naming it never ran. Catch `AggregateException` and read `InnerExceptions`; recovery through `IProjectionRecovery.ReapplyAsync<T>()` is unchanged.
- **BREAKING — a failed `AppendResult` or `CloudAppendResult` no longer reports a version.** `NextExpectedVersion` is now `long?` and is `null` on failure on both types. It used to be `-1`, which is not a spare sentinel — `-1` is the ordinary value meaning *this stream does not exist* — so a caller reading it off a failure got a number asserting the opposite of the truth and could pass it back as an `expectedVersion` to create a stream that already held events. A concurrency conflict still reports a version, because the store measured the stream to detect the conflict.
- **BREAKING — every event store throws `EventTypeNotDeclaredException` for an undeclared event type, and a cancelled append raises `OperationCanceledException`.** If you configure a source-generated JSON type-info resolver you are stating the closed set of event types your process will write; appending outside it is a defect no retry or reload reaches. The new exception derives from `NotSupportedException` and carries the `EventType`, `AggregateId` and `AggregateType`, with the serializer's exception as its inner exception. Separately, five stores caught every exception and turned a cancellation into a failed `AppendResult` — so callers retried inside an already-cancelled scope — while also swallowing programming errors such as `NullReferenceException`. Those catches now name the faults a store can actually raise.

#### Multi-tenancy and isolation

- **BREAKING — a tenant shard that does not declare an isolation coordinate is rejected at resolve time instead of silently falling back to a shared default.** Eight tenant store resolvers filled a missing coordinate from the deployment-wide default: the index prefix and node URI on ElasticSearch and OpenSearch, the container name on Cosmos DB, the database on Firestore and MongoDB, and the schema on Postgres and SQL Server. Two shards each omitting the same coordinate resolved to the same location — on Postgres and SQL Server, to `public` and `dbo` on one server — so tenants a shard map was written to separate shared storage, with no error and nothing in the logs.
- **BREAKING — event store and saga store document keys on Cosmos DB, DynamoDB, Firestore and MongoDB now carry the owning tenant, which changes the stored key shape.** Aggregate identifiers and saga correlation keys come from your domain — an order number, a customer reference — so two tenants arrive at the same identifier as a matter of course. With no tenant term in the key they shared one document and one version counter, and the check that correctly refuses a cross-tenant overwrite was the same check that denied the second tenant a record of its own. The in-memory event store and the in-memory workflow signal inbox are likewise now keyed by tenant, having previously shared one keyspace; entries written before this change are addressed differently now.
- **BREAKING — authorization grants require a tenant, and the four NoSQL grant stores no longer encode an absent tenant as `__null__`.** `Grant.TenantId`, `IActivityGroupGrantStore.InsertActivityGroupGrantAsync` and `IActivityGroupStore.CreateActivityGroupAsync` take a non-nullable `string`, so constructing a grant without a tenant is now a compile error. The Cosmos DB, DynamoDB, Firestore and MongoDB stores translated a null tenant into `__null__` as a partition key, a document id or a key-string fragment — none of which is the framework's reserved untenanted value — so those rows sat in a partition nothing else addressed, and a real tenant named `__null__` shared it. Firestore disagreed with itself: the document id treated an empty string as absent while the stored field treated only null that way, so an empty-tenant grant round-tripped to a different tenant than it was written under. **If you have written grants with no tenant on any of those four providers, they must be backfilled before you upgrade** — a delete-and-reinsert on all four, across two containers each, because the value is part of the partition key or document id rather than an ordinary field. See [Authorization grants require a tenant](https://docs.excalibur-dispatch.dev/docs/migration/authorization-tenant-required).
- **BREAKING — the built-in stores require an `ITenantContext` in their constructors, and on several of them the parameter moved.** Every store that partitions rows by tenant — event, snapshot, inbox, saga, projection, dead-letter and audit stores, including the erasure and legal-hold stores that previously took it as an optional parameter defaulting to `null` — now takes it as a required, non-nullable parameter, so passing `null` no longer compiles. On stores that also accept optional configuration (`schema`, `table`, serializers) the tenant context sits immediately after the required parameters and before the optional ones, because a required parameter cannot follow an optional one. `KeyedTenantPartition.FromContext` and `TenantScope.FromContext` likewise drop their null-accepting overload. A host using the DI extensions needs no change, because `AddMultiTenancy(...)` already registers a context. A store that takes `ITenantContext` in more than one public constructor and is registered through the constructing `AddTenantAwareStore` overload must now mark the intended constructor with `[ActivatorUtilitiesConstructor]`; registration refuses the ambiguity with a message naming the store and the fix, at startup rather than on first resolution.
- **BREAKING — the tenant identifier length accepted at the boundary now matches the width every provider can store, and over-wide tenant columns are narrowed to match.** `TenantIdentityOptions.MaxTenantIdLength` defaulted to 100 while the narrowest tenant column the framework ships is 64 characters, so an identifier of 65 to 100 characters passed inbound validation and then failed at the store — or, on a provider that truncates rather than rejects, silently collided with another tenant — far from the call that supplied it. The default is now 64 and the property is bounded so it cannot be configured above it: raising the bound never made a longer identifier storable, it only moved the failure somewhere harder to diagnose. Auto-provisioning DDL in the compliance erasure, legal-hold and data-inventory stores, the SQL Server materialized-view store, and the published cursor-map and materialized-view schema blocks declared 255 or 256 characters where the migration scripts had already converged on 64. Only newly created tables are affected; an existing column stays as wide as it is, and a wider column stores every identifier the framework now accepts. Documentation and samples were corrected to match.
- **BREAKING — six inbox and outbox administrative operations, and the dead-letter purge, are renamed to state that they reach every tenant. If you call any of them, your build breaks until you rename the call.** Each was already estate-wide in every provider; what changed is the name, not the scope. `IDeadLetterQueueManager.PurgeDeadLetterQueueAsync` becomes **`PurgeAllTenantsDeadLetterQueueAsync`** — it was always unbounded and always crossed every tenant sharing the broker entity, while the same repository already spelt out `AllTenants` on weaker operations, including a read that only *counts*. The statistics operation becomes `GetAllTenantsStatisticsAsync`: outbox and inbox statistics are estate-wide deliberately, because the operation takes no tenant argument, the type it returns carries no tenant field, and the store reads no ambient context, so a confined result would have no way to say which partition it described.
- **BREAKING — a custom `IDeadLetterStore` must now prove it honours the ambient tenant, or multi-tenancy refuses to start.** The contract has always documented itself as tenant-scoped, and it matters more here than almost anywhere else: a dead-letter entry carries the failed message body, so an estate-wide read hands one tenant another tenant's message content. What was missing was any mechanism holding an implementation to it — the requirement was prose, so a store that ignored the ambient tenant was accepted in silence.
- **BREAKING — a relational data request that carries no tenant term must now state which argument makes that correct, and the compiler checks it.** `TenantScope` described *which* partition to address, but nothing recorded whether a request had considered the question, so a request that never mentioned tenancy was indistinguishable from one that had thought about it and correctly concluded it did not apply.
- **`TenantScope.IsSingleTenantPartition` is removed.** It answered "does this scope name the host's one partition rather than one tenant among many", and nothing in the framework asked. Its only known use was a saga purge that refused a real, named tenant on the strength of it — the refusal was the defect, and the fix was to stop consulting it, which left the property shipped and available to the next caller who reasoned that a single-tenant deployment is a special case. Whether a deployment applies tenant confinement at all is a property of the deployment, read from the store's own configuration, not something to infer from the tenant term in hand.
- **Tenant confinement is now documented per provider, because it is not uniform.** The event-sourcing guarantee previously stated that *every* event-store read, write and erase is confined to a single tenant partition. That was false of four shipped stores: **the MongoDB, Cosmos DB, DynamoDB and Firestore event stores did not separate tenants** — their document keys carried no tenant term, so a read under one tenant could return another's events.
- **The compliance data-inventory and Postgres compliance stores read deployment mode the way every other tenant-aware store does, and the in-memory compliance stores write the same tenant term as their SQL siblings.** `PostgresComplianceStore` and the SQL Server, Postgres and in-memory data-inventory stores resolved their partition from whether an `ITenantContext` happened to be *registered* rather than from the deployment-mode flag — and their own registration helpers always register one, so they always bound the single-tenant identity `__default__` and never the reserved untenanted sentinel `__untenanted__`, even on a deployment that never called `AddMultiTenancy(...)`, where every other tenant-aware store binds the sentinel. Separately, the in-memory compliance stores assigned a raw null where the SQL stores fold an absent tenant to the sentinel, so the same input produced two different stored terms depending on the provider; **if you relied on the in-memory store returning a null tenant, it returns the sentinel.** The tenant canonicalisation used when signing an audit chain also has one definition now — the dead-letter replay path re-implemented it by hand in another assembly, with nothing keeping the two in step. No behaviour changes there today; only one implementation is now capable of drifting.

#### Trimming and ahead-of-time compilation

- **BREAKING — ahead-of-time compatibility is claimed only where it is earned, and the claim is enforced.** `IsAotCompatible` and `IsTrimmable` were set for every project by convention, so a package that is not trim-safe shipped an assembly attribute telling your trimmer it was safe to trim code it needs, and the failure landed at your publish rather than our build. Compatibility is now declared per package, trimmability is derived from it by the SDK rather than asserted, and every row of the published compatibility matrix is checked against the project it names on each build — eight rows said "AOT-safe" for packages that declare otherwise, and 39 shipped packages had no row at all. Twenty-three packages that carry annotated reflection paths now read **Annotated**, still usable ahead-of-time with the paths to avoid named, rather than **AOT-safe**. `Excalibur.Jobs.Cdc`, `Excalibur.Jobs.DataProcessing`, `Excalibur.Jobs.SqlServer` and `Excalibur.Hosting.Jobs` withdraw their claim outright, because each ships `Excalibur.Jobs`, which is not compatible. Trim and ahead-of-time analysis remain switched on everywhere, so nothing became less analysed — only the claims moved.
- **The outbox, materialized-view, projection and SQL Server entry-point surfaces now warn at your call site when you publish for trimming or Native AOT.** Every shipped outbox store serializes your payload reflectively and every shipped view or projection store serializes your type reflectively, so `IOutboxStore.EnqueueAsync` and `GetUnsentMessagesAsync`, the fenced-store overload, `IOutboxWriter.WriteAsync`, `IOutboxPublisher`, `IOutboxProcessor`, `IOutboxDispatcher`, `IMaterializedViewStore.GetAsync`/`SaveAsync`, `IAtomicMaterializedViewStore.SaveViewAndPositionAsync`, the four `IMaterializedViewProcessor` methods, `IProjectionStore`, `IPageableProjectionStore`, `ICursorProjectionStore`, `IDistinctValuesProjectionStore`, `IExistsProjectionStore` and the `ProjectionStoreExtensions` helpers are annotated as requiring unreferenced and dynamic code. `AddDispatchWithSqlServer` and both `AddExcaliburSqlServer` overloads carry it too, replacing two blanket suppressions that hid the same requirement inside the package. Behaviour is unchanged and no package withdrew its compatibility claim — the requirement now surfaces where you can act on it instead of being suppressed where no consumer could see it, and a trimmed or AOT publish no longer silently produces a build that fails to round-trip a view. Supply `JsonSerializerOptions` with a source-generated `TypeInfoResolver` for your types to satisfy it. `StageMessageAsync` is deliberately not annotated: it takes an already-serialized message and reflects over nothing.
- **Trim annotations no longer sit on interfaces, and five more APIs declare the requirement they always had.** An annotation on an interface cannot be dropped by an implementation, so a single annotated member forced it onto every implementer and from there onto every caller; twelve interfaces are now clean, the annotation sits on the implementations that genuinely reflect, and your call sites through those interfaces are no longer warned about reflection your code does not reach. `UseZeroAllocation` (which registers a handler invoker that compiles expressions at run time), `DispatchJsonSerializerOptions.Web` (which adds a string-enum converter built at run time) and the health-check response options derived from them now carry `[RequiresDynamicCode]`, and `RegisterMapper<TMapper>`, `AddTenantOptions<TOptions>` and `MediatRCompatOptions.AddBehavior` state the members they need preserved under trimming. Nothing behaves differently at run time — the diagnostics moved to your build.
- **Trim suppressions that were never suppressing anything are gone.** A `#pragma warning disable` silences the compiler's analyzer, not the ahead-of-time compiler, so several call sites still raised the diagnostic at publish while the source read as though it had been handled. Where the call really was reflective the requirement is now declared; where it was not — a trace exporter annotated for a JSON call it no longer makes, and a cached-value converter reaching for `JsonSerializer.Deserialize<JsonElement>` where `JsonElement.ParseValue` reads the same value without reflection — the reflection itself is gone.
- **`OutboxStoreConformanceTestKit` declares that it is not trim-safe, and the conformance kits declare the reflection they perform over your test class.** The outbox kit reads staged messages back through the store under test, which deserializes reflectively, so a suite deriving from it must carry the same annotation when built with the trim analyzer enabled; it no longer claims its methods are reflection-preserved, a claim that could not coexist with the requirement. A trimmed test host was never a supported configuration for this kit. Every kit checks that your derived class exposes each arm by reflecting over its own runtime type, and now states that requirement so a trimmed build preserves those members instead of dropping them and leaving the kit to report your suite as unwired.

#### Registration, hosting and defaults

- **BREAKING — handlers discovered by assembly scanning are registered `Transient` instead of `Scoped`, and this does not reduce what you can rely on.** You never needed a `Scoped` registration in order to depend on a scoped service: Dispatch decides whether to open a dependency-injection scope by inspecting the handler's constructor dependency graph, not by reading the lifetime you registered it with. A transient handler that depends on `IUnitOfWork` still gets a scope in every host — ASP.NET Core, worker, console and serverless alike — and one that reaches no scoped dependency now stops paying for a scope it cannot use, removing 472 bytes of allocation per dispatch on a dependency-free handler. The `lifetime:` parameter is unchanged, so `ServiceLifetime.Scoped` remains available where you want the handler instance itself tied to the scope. The default moved on `AddHandlersFromAssembly`, `AddHandler<TMessage, THandler>` and the generated `AddDiscoveredHandlers` together, so reflection-based and source-generated consumers do not diverge.
- **Handler registration no longer discards handlers.** Three defects fixed together: `AddDispatch(assembly)` registered handler interfaces first-wins, so when two handlers subscribed to the same event only one was ever wired — on a documented fan-out contract, with no error; registering your own handler *before* `AddDispatch(...)` let assembly scanning overwrite it, though documentation stated both orderings were safe; and `registerWithContainer: false` suppressed only the concrete registration, leaving interface registrations behind, which combined with the ordering defect to run a handler you did not choose. Event handlers now register additively while single-handler message types keep last-wins, using the same rule the handler registry itself applies, so the container and the registry can no longer disagree about whether a message type fans out; either ordering runs your handler; and opting out now registers nothing from that assembly, which is what its name says.
- **Scoped dependencies are no longer shared across dispatches.** Four related defects. A handler registered `Scoped` or `Singleton` was resolved as a single shared instance anyway, by a stateless-handler optimisation applied without checking the lifetime you registered — and the option documented as controlling it gated a separate startup-time path, so disabling it changed nothing; the optimisation now applies only to `Transient`, `AutoPromoteStatelessHandlersToSingleton` disables it everywhere, and Dispatch logs one advisory per handler type (`Information`, event ID `40908`) where `Transient` would be cheaper. A caller-supplied root provider from `BuildServiceProvider()` — the natural shape in a worker or console app — was accepted as if it were a scope, and is now rejected in favour of a real one. `PublishAsync` and `SendDocumentAsync` bypassed the scope decision entirely, so in a worker, console or serverless host their handlers received a shared instance regardless of registered lifetime; all dispatch paths now go through the same decision, and all handlers for a single published event share one scope, so an event is handled as one unit of work — note that this shares state between sibling handlers even though faults remain isolated. And a keyed descriptor recorded under its bare service type could mask a non-keyed scoped registration, because a keyed descriptor is reachable only through its key: `AddKeyedSingleton<IFoo, Foo>("x")` after `AddScoped<IFoo, Foo>()` made Dispatch conclude the handler was root-safe, so every dispatch shared one instance of a service you had registered as scoped — order-dependent and silent, just a `DbContext` shared across messages. Keyed descriptors are now excluded from that lookup.
- **BREAKING — registering only an outbox or a saga no longer turns on stateless-handler singleton promotion for you.** Those registrations bootstrapped the dispatch core through the zero-argument `AddDispatch()`, which set `AutoPromoteStatelessHandlersToSingleton` to `true` as a side effect. They now bootstrap through `AddDispatchPipeline()`, which registers the same primitives and leaves the option at its documented default of `false`. If you registered persistence but never called `AddDispatch` yourself, your stateless handlers were being promoted to singletons implicitly and will now be constructed per dispatch. A composition that calls `AddDispatch` is unaffected.
- **BREAKING — a host that is misconfigured at startup now fails to start, where it previously started and ran broken.** Three conditions that were logged and stepped over are now startup failures: no `IDispatcher` registered, the outbox enabled with no store registered, and a keyed default that cannot be resolved. **The outbox case is the one to read twice** — a host in that state previously started, reported healthy, and dropped every message it staged, which its own operators would usually discover from their customers rather than from a log. Each failure names the missing registration and the method that supplies it. Startup prerequisite checks now also run without a generic host: `ValidateStartupGates()` was documented as running them and ran only the options gates, so a container built by hand — a custom serverless runtime, a manual `BuildServiceProvider()` — returned cleanly and failed later at first use. Two checks that require I/O remain host-only and are named in the documentation along with their fallbacks.
- **BREAKING — a host that starts scheduled delivery on the volatile in-memory schedule store is refused at startup, and a durable store now replaces the in-memory one regardless of composition order.** `AddTimeAwareScheduling`, `AddAdaptiveTimeAwareScheduling`, `AddLightweightTimeAwareScheduling` and `AddThroughputTimeAwareScheduling` install a boot-time durability gate; unless a durable schedule store has been registered, startup fails with an `OptionsValidationException` explaining what would be lost and naming both ways forward. `AddDurableScheduleStore` previously registered with `TryAdd`, so if scheduling had already been composed the durable registration was a silent no-op while the durability attestation was still emitted — a host that asked for a durable store therefore passed the gate and ran on the volatile one. The store and its attestation are now inseparable in either order. **If you register both, verify which store you are actually on**: before this release, `AddDispatchScheduling()` followed by `AddDurableScheduleStore<T>()` left you on the in-memory store with no indication.
- **BREAKING — a host that registers a leader election but leaves the outbox drain ungated now refuses to start.** Previously it started silently and every instance drained the outbox concurrently. The startup check keyed on the presence of the *gate* — the very component it exists to require — so a host that registered an election through a path that never wired the gate resolved no gate, read as single-instance, and passed. It now keys on the presence of the *election*, and refuses when nothing fences the drain. If this stops your host, you have been running unfenced: wire the gate with the outbox builder's `WithLeaderElection()`, or, if exactly one process drains that outbox, declare it with `AsSingleWriter()` and take on the single-active-writer guarantee yourself. A host with no leader election registered is unaffected.
- **BREAKING — the Google Pub/Sub transport options no longer carry a CloudEvents property.** Configuring CloudEvents through `ConfigureCloudEvents` now hands your delegate to the CloudEvents options registration directly, as the Azure Service Bus transport already did, so the value reaches the adapter that reads it. The property on the transport options was written and never read, so a host setting it saw CloudEvents configuration that had no effect. **If you assigned it directly, that assignment no longer compiles; move the configuration into `ConfigureCloudEvents`,** which now works.
- **BREAKING — the cloud batch-operation types have moved out of the Cosmos DB package.** `CloudBatchCreateOperation`, `CloudBatchReplaceOperation`, `CloudBatchUpsertOperation` and `CloudBatchDeleteOperation` now live in **Excalibur.Data.Abstractions** under `Excalibur.Data.CloudNative`. They were provider-neutral all along, and their old home meant the Firestore and DynamoDB providers could not see them — see *Fixed* for what that cost. Most code needs no change, because the namespace they moved into was already imported for `ICloudBatchOperation`.
- **BREAKING — `IJobOptions` and `JobOptions` properties are settable rather than init-only.** The configuration binding source generator cannot bind init-only properties and emits no diagnostic when it skips them, so options bound that way would silently become their defaults. If you implement `IJobOptions`, change your `init` accessors to `set`. `CdcJobOptions.DatabaseConfigs` is likewise no longer `required`; validation still rejects an empty collection.
- **BREAKING — the in-memory provider no longer writes to disk when you dispose it.** With `PersistToDisk` configured, `Dispose()` and `DisposeAsync()` previously tried to flush before tearing down; they now tear down only, and log a warning telling you the flush was skipped. Persisting is an explicit call: **`InMemoryPersistenceProvider.PersistToDiskAsync`**, which is now public. Loading on initialization is unchanged. If you relied on disposal to save, call `PersistToDiskAsync` before disposing. The old behaviour was never dependable in the synchronous path anyway — it started the write on a background task nobody waited for, so a process exiting promptly after `Dispose()` could lose it silently — and disposal that requires reflection cannot be used from a trimmed or Native AOT application at all.
- **BREAKING — `IPersistenceProvider.ExecuteAsync<TConnection, TResult>` is removed; executing an `IDataRequest` is an optional capability rather than a member every provider must have.** It required a connection type the caller named while the provider supplied the connection, which no provider could honour: five threw `NotSupportedException`, three threw `InvalidCastException` from an unchecked cast, and the rest accepted exactly one connection type and rejected the rest at runtime. Separately, `IPersistenceProvider.Name` now reports the instance you configured rather than the engine it runs on — SQL Server, PostgreSQL, Elasticsearch and OpenSearch returned a fixed label regardless of configuration, and SQL Server returned its own class name — so all eleven providers return the `Name` you set on their options, falling back to the provider's registration key (`"sqlserver"`, `"postgres"`, `"elasticsearch"`, `"opensearch"`, `"mysql"`, `"mongodb"`, `"redis"`, `"inmemory"`, `"cosmosdb"`, `"dynamodb"`, `"firestore"`). Two instances of the same engine are now distinguishable. `IPersistenceProviderHealth.IsAvailable` is documented in terms you can act on — it was described circularly as *"true if the provider is available and healthy"* — and now states the contract implementations keep: `true` while the provider is ready to serve and has not been disposed, and a cheap non-blocking flag that performs no I/O.
- **`InputValidationMiddleware` composes without security auditing.** `ISecurityEventLogger` is a telemetry sink for the control rather than the control itself — validation runs, and rejects, whether or not an audit trail is configured — but it was required, so `AddInputValidation` on its own produced a container that **could not enumerate its own dispatch pipeline**. The dependency is now optional; every event the middleware would have audited is still written to `ILogger`, so nothing is dropped silently.
- **Postgres persistence options report nested validation failures through the parent message.** A range violation on `Connection`, `Statements`, `Pooling` or `Resilience` now surfaces under `"Postgres persistence options validation failed: ..."`; the per-section wrapper text no longer appears. The exception type is unchanged and more rules are enforced than before, because the nested annotations are now checked by construction rather than by each section remembering to validate itself. Only assertions on the exact message string are affected.
- **`Excalibur.Compliance` moves to QuestPDF 2026.7.3** from 2026.2.4. Two notes the package's own comments previously got wrong: QuestPDF is **not** MIT-licensed — its Community edition is free only for licensees below a total annual gross revenue threshold, and above that threshold a paid licence is required — and the PDF export path is **not** covered by any ahead-of-time compilation validation we run, so treat it as unvalidated under Native AOT even though the package declares AOT compatibility for its other paths.

#### Caching

- **BREAKING — `CacheTimeout` now bounds the distributed cache backend, not your handler.** It was applied around the whole cache operation, and the cache runs your handler inside that operation to populate a miss, so the deadline bounded handler execution too. Because every concurrent caller armed the same deadline, a handler slower than `CacheTimeout` caused all of them to abandon the shared work at the same instant and each run the handler itself: five concurrent callers of one cacheable query invoked the handler **six** times with the 200 ms default and a 400 ms handler. That is worse than no caching, and it fell on exactly the slow operations caching exists to protect, silently, because skipping the cache on failure is by design.
- **Caching no longer runs a handler twice when the handler itself throws, no longer deadlocks when registered twice, and reports cache-key re-entry instead of hanging.** The fail-open path exists so a cache-backend failure cannot become an application failure, but it wrapped the value factory that *invokes your handler*, so a fault your handler raised and a dead cache backend arrived at the same place — and a non-idempotent handler performed its side effect **twice for one dispatch** purely because caching was enabled. A backend that fails fast still falls open and still runs the handler exactly once. `WithCachingOptions` routes through `UseCaching`, so the documented `UseCaching().WithCachingOptions(...)` pairing registered the middleware twice, and two caching stages deadlock: the outer stage's cache population runs the rest of the pipeline into the inner stage, which asks for the entry the outer stage is still creating. Registration is now idempotent, as both methods already documented. And a cached handler that re-enters its own cache key now throws an `InvalidOperationException` naming the key rather than waiting for good — the per-request deadline used to mask this by turning it into a timeout and a duplicate execution.

#### Observability

- **BREAKING — metric instruments declare UCUM units, which changes the Prometheus series names they export, and six instruments carrying their unit in their name were renamed.** 244 instruments declared a free-text unit such as `count`, `messages`, `milliseconds` or `deadlocks`; the Prometheus exporter appends an unrecognised unit to the series name verbatim, so `postgres.deadlocks.total` was exported as `postgres_deadlocks_total_deadlocks_total` and `dispatch.messages.processed` as `dispatch_messages_processed_count_total`. Dashboards and alerts written against the documented names matched nothing and showed an empty panel with no error. The six renames change the **OpenTelemetry instrument name**, so they affect any backend that keys on it, not only Prometheus — a name ending in `_ms` collected the expanded unit as a second suffix, exporting `dispatch_saga_duration_ms_milliseconds`. Transport send and receive duration are also reported by one instrument instead of two: the duplicate pair measured the same quantity and could both be active in one process, so a dashboard aggregating either one double-counted or picked between them arbitrarily depending on which telemetry seam you had enabled. The surviving names no longer carry a unit suffix.
- **BREAKING — metric instruments that nothing recorded to have been removed, including the Postgres connection-pool metrics.** Three collectors registered instruments no code path ever wrote to, so a consumer who subscribed to their meter received no data points and had no way to tell that from an idle system: `IPersistenceMetrics`, `IPersistenceMetricsAdmin`, `PersistenceMetricsExtensions` and `PersistenceMetricsSnapshot` are removed from `Excalibur.Data.Abstractions` along with the `Excalibur.Data.Persistence` meter and activity source, and `IQueueMetricsCollector`, `IQueueMetricsCollectorAdmin` and `QueueMetricsCollector` from `Excalibur.Dispatch.Transport.AzureServiceBus`. None of them was registered by any `Add…` extension. Separately, `postgres.connections.active`, `postgres.connections.idle`, `postgres.pool.size` and `postgres.pool.utilization`, together with the matching keys of `PostgresPersistenceMetrics.GetCurrentMetrics()`, reported a constant zero: the method that supplied their values had no caller, and it was fed by a path that read `pg_stat_activity`, which describes every client of the server rather than this process's pool. A dashboard reading any of the four was being told there were no connections, not that the figure was unavailable. For real pool figures use the metrics Npgsql itself emits; `postgres.prepared.statements` and every other instrument on that type are unaffected.
- **Provider metrics can be attributed to a provider.** `GetMetricsAsync` is required to report which implementation answered, but the dictionary is compared ordinally and Elasticsearch, OpenSearch and Postgres spelled the key `provider` or omitted it, so a consumer reading the documented `Provider` key found nothing. All three now report it and their remaining keys use the PascalCase the other providers already used, and the requirement is stated on the interface rather than only enforced by the conformance suite. **If you read these dictionaries by key, the changed names are** `provider`, `providerType`, `indexPrefix`, `isAvailable`, `clusterStatus`, `numberOfNodes`, `activeShards`, `healthCheckError`, `type`, `database_type`, `database_version`, `available` and `initialized`.

#### Conformance kits

- **BREAKING — eleven conformance kits now fail a deriving suite that does not expose every arm to its test runner, and several gained arms.** This is breaking if you derive `KeyEscrowServiceConformanceTestKit`, `MasterKeyBackupServiceConformanceTestKit`, `EncryptionProviderConformanceTestKit`, `EncryptionMigrationServiceConformanceTestKit`, `LegalHoldStoreConformanceTestKit`, `DataInventoryStoreConformanceTestKit`, `ScheduleStoreConformanceTestKit`, `StreamingHandlerConformanceTestKit`, `DeduplicatorConformanceTestKit` or `InboxStoreConformanceTestKit`: a suite that omits an arm now fails with the arm named, where before it passed silently while never running that check. New arms: three lease-reclaim arms and `ConcurrentClaimAndMark_MustElectExactlyOneWinner_AndKeepTheProcessedMarker` on the inbox kit, taking it from 22 arms to 23; a concurrent claim-and-sweep arm on the deduplicator kit; and `OutboxStoreConformanceTestKit.OwnershipSuite_ShouldExerciseThisStoreOrNotDeclareIt`, which fails a suite that declares a store it never exercises, so a conformance run cannot report a pass for a backend it did not touch. Declare a member per arm — a wrapper that calls it, or an override — and attribute it for your runner.
- **Kits record an arm they skip instead of passing silently, and three no longer demand a test wrapper around a lifecycle hook.** An arm that could not find the capability it needed returned early, and a skipped arm was indistinguishable from a passing one; `ConformanceArmLedger` now records every arm that executes and every arm that skips, with the capability and the reason. The outbox kit also discovers capabilities through `GetService`, which the contract requires, rather than by casting the store — a cast sees only the outermost type, so a decorated store failed the check and its fencing arms reported as passes. `CleanupAsync` is a lifecycle hook with a working default, called after every arm and documented as an override point, but the wiring guard counted it as a conformance arm, so derivers of `KeyManagementProviderConformanceTestKit`, `ClaimCheckProviderConformanceTestKit` and `CacheTagTrackerConformanceTestKit` no longer need a wrapper around it.
- **The event-store and saga kits declare multi-tenant mode alongside the tenant context they register, the saga kit registers logging, and the persistence kit accepts a declined optional capability.** The event-store and saga kits supply a tenant-bearing context and change the partition between operations — which is a multi-tenant deployment — while leaving the deployment mode at its single-tenant default, and that pairing is the one the framework refuses at startup: a store whose startup schema handshake reads the mode converges the untenanted partition onto the single-tenant identity, moving the arms' own rows out from under them. `SagaStoreConformanceTestKit` built a bare `ServiceCollection` while every shipped saga store resolves `ILogger<TStore>` with `GetRequiredService`, so a deriver failed on a missing logger before reaching any conformance arm. And `PersistenceProviderConformanceTestKit` threw on the `null` that `IPersistenceProvider.GetService` is specified to return for an unsupported optional capability, so a provider giving the documented answer was reported non-conforming; arms that previously failed now pass with no change to your provider, while one that advertises a capability and then throws still fails.

#### Transports

- **BREAKING — the Kafka and RabbitMQ transports refuse to build a connection that would run in the clear.** If you register Kafka today and connect to a broker over `PLAINTEXT` or `SASL_PLAINTEXT` — including a local broker, a Docker Compose broker, or a test container — your application will now fail where the transport is wired rather than connecting. RabbitMQ likewise refuses a registration that carried no TLS and used to succeed. Both transports have always applied the TLS settings you gave them; what they could not do was refuse when you gave them none, because the only code that checked whether a connection was actually encrypted lived in a connection class nothing on the registration path constructs — so the check was correct, tested, and unreachable, and a consumer who required TLS had no way to make the transport insist on it.
- **The RabbitMQ transport honours `AcceptUntrustedCertificates` and applies one certificate posture to both ways of enabling TLS.** The setting was read by nothing, so a broker with a self-signed certificate failed the handshake with no way to proceed. It now waives exactly two validation errors — a certificate that does not chain to a trusted root, and one whose subject does not match the host being dialled. A broker that presents no certificate at all is still refused.
- **Four transports apply your configuration delegate directly to the options they resolve, instead of copying it field by field.** The Azure Service Bus, Pulsar, Google Pub/Sub and AWS SNS registrations each built a private options instance from your builder calls and then assigned its properties one at a time onto the options the container actually serves, so a property the builder could set but the assignment list omitted was collected and silently discarded, with nothing about the call site revealing which properties were in that state. The builder is now a view over the instance the options system owns, so there is no second instance and no list to fall out of step. **No action is required, and nothing you have already configured changes meaning**; the behaviour that changes is that a value you set is now guaranteed to arrive.
- **The Consul leader-election provider enforces the configured grace period.** It previously read the setting from nowhere and diverged from the bound in both directions: a single failed read of the leader key dropped leadership immediately, with no grace at all, while a renewal that threw left the candidate reporting leadership indefinitely — past the point where Consul's own session TTL had lapsed and another candidate could hold the lock. Both paths are now measured against the grace period, from the last exchange with Consul that confirmed this candidate holds the lock; a response in which Consul states the session is gone is definitive and still relinquishes at once. Grace defaults to 5 seconds; set `GracePeriod` to the window your protected resource can tolerate.

#### Documentation and schema corrections

- **BREAKING — the shipped SQL Server and PostgreSQL migrations no longer require a specific client to run.** Every SQL Server migration opened with `:on error exit`, the outbox schema declared its names with `:setvar` and referenced them as `$(OutboxTable)`, and six PostgreSQL migrations carried `\set ON_ERROR_STOP on`. These are `sqlcmd` and `psql` *client* commands, not SQL, so SSMS outside SQLCMD mode, Azure Data Studio, DbUp, Flyway, Liquibase, EF migrations, Npgsql, JDBC and any connection loop of your own sent them to the server and got `Msg 102, Incorrect syntax near ':'` or `42601: syntax error at or near "\"` on the script's first line, having applied nothing. Because the outbox store creates no tables at runtime, there was no other way to provision the outbox on those hosts — and with no tables, nothing is delivered. The SQL Server directive is replaced by plain T-SQL that does the same job in every client: each batch now opens with `IF @@TRANCOUNT = 0 SET NOEXEC ON;`, so once a refusal has rolled the transaction back, every later batch is sent but never executed and the database is not left half-migrated. **All of these now apply on any driver, unchanged and without preprocessing.** The check meant to keep client meta-commands out of shipped scripts could not fail — it asserted on text returned by the test helper that *strips* those commands, so it proved the helper copes with a directive rather than that the shipped file runs — and it now asserts against the bytes each script actually ships, so a re-added `\set`, `:setvar` or `:on error exit` fails immediately without needing a database.
- **Three shipped migrations that had no tests now have them, and three tenant-column changes now reach existing databases.** `AuditLogging.Postgres/Scripts/002_NarrowTenantIdToPortableMaximum.sql`, `EventSourcing.Postgres/Scripts/007_NarrowSnapshotTenantIdToPortableMaximum.sql` and `Saga.Oracle/Scripts/01-SagaSchema.Upgrade.sql` are covered against real PostgreSQL and Oracle: each converges a database at the prior version, is safe to run a second time, leaves a table it does not recognise alone, and — the property these exist for — **refuses rather than truncating** when a stored tenant identifier is longer than the column it is being narrowed to. No behaviour changed; these were already correct, and are now proven so. Truncating would merge two tenants that agree in their first 64 characters, and for the snapshot and saga tables that column is part of the primary key, so the merge would be a key collision rather than a mislabelling. Separately, `Excalibur.AuditLogging.Postgres`, `Excalibur.EventSourcing.Postgres` and `Excalibur.Saga.Oracle` changed the shape of their tenant column in the create script, which guards the table with an existence check — so a database provisioned by an earlier version kept the old shape and nothing reported it. Each package now ships the migration alongside its create script; running it against a database that already has the target shape does nothing, so it is safe to run twice or on a fresh install. If you overrode the default schema or table name, edit the literals named in each script's header.
- **The documented snapshot and event-store schemas no longer contradict the scripts we ship.** The snapshots page inlined a copy of the SQL Server `EventStoreSnapshots` table declaring `CreatedAt` as `DATETIME2` — with a comment arguing that `DATETIMEOFFSET` would not round-trip — while the shipped `002_CreateSnapshotSchema.sql` declares `DATETIMEOFFSET`, which is what the store reads; a consumer who provisioned from the page got a table whose `CreatedAt` matches no constructor, so every snapshot read failed outright rather than degrading. The same copy had drifted on column widths, `SnapshotId` nullability, the primary key's name and a missing index. The page no longer reproduces the schema at all: it points at the script shipped in each provider package, and now lists Oracle and PostgreSQL alongside SQL Server — both have shipped for some time and the page named only SQL Server, so there was no published Oracle snapshot schema to follow. The event-store setup guide had the same defect on the snapshot table, plus key columns a character wider than the shipped script's and a snapshot primary key exceeding SQL Server's clustered index key limit. And `002_CreateSnapshotSchema.sql` explained its own `NOT NULL` tenant column by contrasting it with an events table "which stays nullable to keep pre-tenancy rows reachable" — not the shape the package ships, and read as current guidance it would lead you to leave the events tenant column nullable and skip `004_MakeEventTenantTotal.sql`. No table shape changed; only the explanation did.
- **The outbox, metrics, source-generator and inbox documentation no longer teach code that does not work.** Five places across four pages showed `excalibur.AddOutbox(OutboxOptions.Balanced().Build())` and its `HighThroughput` / `HighReliability` / `Custom` siblings, but `AddOutbox` has exactly one public overload and it takes a configuration callback — a reader following the preset examples did not get a misconfigured outbox, they got code that would not build. Sixteen rows of the metrics reference named Postgres persistence and outbox instruments that no instrument emits: five near-misses in the persistence table, an entire `postgres.outbox.*` table that has never been emitted (those instruments are `excalibur.outbox.*` on the `Excalibur.Outbox.Postgres` meter, which the `Excalibur.Data.*` wildcard in the documented quick start does **not** match), and a documented `postgres_pool_utilization` query referring to no instrument at all — the framework registers no connection-pool gauge. The source-generator reference listed output files no generator emits, misattributed `PrecompiledHandlerMetadata.g.cs` and its runtime-query API to the wrong generator, and listed only some of the files two generators produce, so anyone who went looking for a generated file by the documented name did not find it. Documentation showing `options.ConfigureInbox(...)` is corrected — that call would not have compiled.
- **The getting-started query example compiles.** `Excalibur.Dispatch` now bundles its source generator, so `var result = await dispatcher.DispatchAsync(action, ct); result.ReturnValue` works as documented. The generator emits the typed `DispatchAsync` overload that carries the response type; without it the call bound to the non-generic `IMessageResult` and failed with `CS1061` on `.ReturnValue`. The generator was referenced but never packed, while the sibling analyzer was — so the inference worked on our build and not on yours. Installing it separately is no longer necessary, and every consumer is now on the zero-reflection handler-resolution path by default.
- **The saga architecture document no longer requires globally unique saga identifiers.** It stated that identifiers must be unique across tenants because the idempotency record carried no tenant term. That record was never live code, so the obligation was never enforced and is now obsolete on its own terms: the saga row binds its tenant at creation and the load filters on it, so two tenants may safely present the same saga identifier. No replacement guarantee is asserted in its place — the durability contract for saga event de-duplication is a bounded, best-effort window, and stating it precisely is open work. A written gap is better than an invented guarantee.

### Added

- **Oracle, Postgres and compliance packages now ship the schema their stores need.** `Excalibur.Outbox.Oracle` carried no DDL in any form and `Excalibur.Saga.Oracle` shipped only its timeout tables, not the sagas table itself. `Excalibur.EventSourcing.Oracle` shipped a script for the snapshot table — an optimisation — and none for `EVENTSTOREEVENTS`, the table the event store exists to write to, so every append failed with `ORA-00942` until the table was reconstructed by reading our source. `Excalibur.Outbox.Postgres` staged messages to `outbox`, `outbox_dead_letters` and `outbox_fence` and gave you no way to create any of them. Six GDPR erasure, data-inventory and legal-hold stores across `Excalibur.Compliance.SqlServer` and `Excalibur.Compliance.Postgres` verify their schema on startup and throw telling you to *"create the schema out of band"*, with no script in either package to follow. In each case the only complete definition lived in a test fixture, which is ours and not yours — so following our own instructions produced `ORA-00904`, `42703 column does not exist`, or an instruction that could not be carried out. All of these now ship a packed script under `scripts/`, and each README points at it rather than inlining a copy that would drift from it. Four further packages — the Postgres change data capture state store, the SQLite event store, and the Postgres and SQL Server leader-election health stores — carried their `CREATE TABLE` scripts in the source tree but not in the published package, and now include them too. The rule that packages them is declared once for every provider, so a provider added later cannot ship without its scripts.
- **`AddOpenSearchIndexManagement()` registers the OpenSearch index management services.** `IIndexLifecycleManager`, `IIndexTemplateManager`, `IIndexOperationsManager` and `IIndexAliasManager` were public and documented as resolvable from the container, and no entry point registered any of them — `GetRequiredService<IIndexLifecycleManager>()` threw. Call it after registering a client; it accepts either client registration shape, the `IOpenSearchClient` interface or the concrete `OpenSearchClient` this package's own entry points produce, and resolving a manager with no client in the container throws naming the registration you are missing rather than quietly building a client aimed at a default local address.
- **`ILeasedInboxStore` separates the self-expiring lease protocol from the caller-governed claim.** `IClaimableInboxStore` carried both — a claim whose TTL you govern, and a lease that expires on its own — the latter as a default implementation that threw, and the standard inbox path selected the lease purely by a type check, so **the DynamoDB, Firestore and Elasticsearch inbox stores threw `NotSupportedException` on the standard deduplication path.** They declare the claim capability and do not implement the lease, and the startup guard could not tell the difference because it probed one boolean across both protocols. Lease support is now its own interface and its own capability flag, so a store that cannot lease is never routed onto the lease path and a caller can ask before it calls; the dispatch path gates on the capability rather than the declared interface, because a decorator declares the interfaces it forwards and a type check therefore passes for a decorator whose inner store cannot serve the protocol.
- **Every event store accepts your own `IJsonTypeInfoResolver`, and `AddAotEventSerializer` is public.** The in-memory store plus the Cosmos DB, DynamoDB, Firestore, MongoDB, Oracle, PostgreSQL, Redis, SQLite and SQL Server providers each built their own serializer options, so a source-generated context registered in your container was never consulted and an ahead-of-time publish failed at runtime; `InMemoryEventStoreOptions.EventTypeInfoResolver` is the in-memory store's knob. Reflection remains the default and output is byte-identical when you supply nothing. `AddAotEventSerializer` registers the ahead-of-time event serializer with your source-generated `JsonSerializerContext` — the serializer existed but was internal and unregistered, while shipped surfaces including a runtime exception message told you to use it. Note that event metadata is a dictionary of `object`, so declare the closed types your metadata values use; declaring the dictionary type itself compiles and then fails at runtime.
- **The OpenSearch projection store validates its options.** `OpenSearchProjectionStoreOptions` had a validator that nothing registered, so an unparseable `NodeUri`, a non-positive `RequestTimeoutSeconds`, or a shard count below one reached the client instead of failing where you configured it. `AddOpenSearchProjectionStore<T>(...)` now registers it.
- **`FirestoreSnapshotStoreOptions` exposes the contended-write retry budget.** `MaxContendedWriteAttempts` (16) and `ContendedWriteBackoffMilliseconds` (25) were hardcoded constants. The number means what it says — the store's own loop is the only retry on the path, so setting it to four attempts a contended write four times.
- **The Cosmos DB event store lets you name the database.** `CosmosDbEventStoreOptions.DatabaseName` is now settable rather than fixed, so the store can live in a database you choose.
- **The DynamoDB event store no longer requires a change-feed client, and you can supply your own.** The store had come to demand an `IAmazonDynamoDBStreams` that no registration path provided, so the container could not construct it at all — an event store used without a change feed failed on resolution. A streams client is a change-feed concern, not an event-store one, so it is now optional; `StreamsClient(...)` and `StreamsClientFactory(...)` on the DynamoDB event-sourcing builder let you supply your own so it runs under the credentials, endpoint and telemetry you configured rather than an ambient default. A DynamoDB provider built from your own `IAmazonDynamoDB` also gains a constructor overload taking an `IAmazonDynamoDBStreams` alongside your document client — the client-only constructor marked the provider initialized without ever creating the streams client, and the change-feed path then dereferenced that field regardless. Both clients stay yours to dispose, and creating a change-feed subscription on the client-only constructor now fails immediately naming the overload to use instead.
- **The Consul leader-election builder gains `ResourceName(...)`**, without which that path could not name the resource it elects on.

### Fixed

#### Delivery correctness — outbox, inbox and deduplication

- **BREAKING — the Firestore and Elasticsearch inbox stores produced colliding document ids, and correcting it changes the stored id.** Both joined the tenant, message id and handler type with `_` without separating a term that contains `_` from the separator between terms, so tenant `acme` with message `corp_42` and tenant `acme_corp` with message `42` addressed one document — and the second message was read as an already-seen duplicate and dropped, silently, on the success path. The ambiguity also defeated the reserved untenanted term, whose entire purpose is to be unmistakable: `__untenanted__` with message `a` and the real tenant `__untenanted` with message `_a` addressed the same document, so an untenanted entry and a tenanted one could deduplicate against each other. Each term is now percent-encoded and joined with `:`, which distinct messages cannot collide under. **This requires action if you run the inbox on either store:** entries written by an earlier version are addressed by the old id and are not found by this version, and the store deliberately does not fall back — a fallback would keep the ambiguous form alive indefinitely — so drain the inbox before upgrading, or re-key the existing entries. On Firestore the new id additionally carries a constant `inbox:` prefix, because Firestore rejects document ids matching `__.*__`; the same encoding escapes `/`, which Firestore reads as a path separator and which previously sent an entry to a path the matching read never looked in. Two writes that previously succeeded can now be refused, loudly and at composition time rather than silently: an id exceeding the store's document-id limit (512 bytes on Elasticsearch — percent-encoding expands, and a nested closed generic handler type name grows by roughly a quarter, so long handler type names that fitted before may not now), and a term containing an unpaired surrogate, which the encoder would otherwise fold onto a shared replacement character and merge with another message. Neither is ever resolved by truncating the key. Both procedures, and what to check before upgrading, are in [Firestore and Elasticsearch inbox keys change shape](https://docs.excalibur-dispatch.dev/docs/migration/inbox-document-id-rekey). No other inbox provider changed.
- **Four outbox stores decided whether a lease had expired by comparing two machines' clocks.** MongoDB, Redis, Elasticsearch and Marten compared a lease timestamp written by one dispatcher against a cutoff computed from another dispatcher's clock. Where two dispatcher hosts disagreed about the time by more than the lease timeout, the one running ahead read a live lease as expired and claimed a message the other was still delivering — with no crash, no pause and no elapsed time. The atomic claim underneath did not prevent this and could not: it decides which of two *simultaneous* claimants wins, and under clock skew the two are not simultaneous, so the second dispatcher's write was the only one at that instant and succeeded on a condition that was already false. Each store now evaluates both sides of that comparison on one clock every dispatcher shares — `clock_timestamp()` on Marten, `$$NOW` on MongoDB, `redis.call('TIME')` on Redis, and a node-side conditional update on Elasticsearch. **If you run more than one dispatcher against any of those four stores you were exposed to duplicate concurrent delivery proportional to your clock skew, and no longer are.** The **Oracle** and **Elasticsearch** failure-anchored retry floor had the same fault in the other direction — the floor is what keeps a failing destination from being hot-looped at poll cadence, and one side of the comparison was produced somewhere other than where it was read, so the retry was delayed or advanced by exactly however far the two machines disagreed, quietly, and a store that never hands a due message back satisfies every safety property while delivering nothing. Handlers must still be idempotent: the outbox is at-least-once by design.
- **The MongoDB outbox reads both durable shapes of a stored instant, so a message staged by an earlier version is judged correctly.** This package now writes instants as BSON dates; a message staged by a previously published version is on disk in the driver's default `{ DateTime, Ticks, Offset }` sub-document shape, and nothing rewrites it. The two are not interchangeable to the server: BSON's canonical type ordering places every sub-document **below** every date, so an aggregation comparison answered such a field the same way at every instant — **a lease written by an older dispatcher read as expired always**, and during a rolling upgrade a newer dispatcher would claim a message an older one was still delivering, then re-claim it on every subsequent poll, a duplicate window bounded by nothing. Query operators are type-bracketed and fail the opposite way, hiding such a message from a comparison rather than always matching it, which is how a scheduled or sent message could be passed over indefinitely. Every site that compares an instant now reads both shapes. **No action is required and no migration is needed** — mixed collections are handled, and messages keep their existing shape until they are next written.
- **Two SQL Server outbox clock defects: a message whose backoff had elapsed could fail to come back, and one could stay invisible for as long as the dispatcher's clock trailed the database's.** Three adjacent predicates in the claim used two different clock functions — `SYSUTCDATETIME()` (accurate to about 100 nanoseconds) for scheduled-due and next-attempt, `GETUTCDATE()` (about 3.33 milliseconds) for the lease — and the lease comparison is strict, so a claim and a reclaim landing inside the same tick compared equal and the lease read as not yet expired, so **a message that should have been retried was not returned** until some later poll fell in a different tick. Separately, due-ness was decided against an instant supplied by the dispatcher while the failure backoff writes `NextAttemptAt` from the database's own clock — deliberately, so a dispatcher with a fast clock cannot shorten the backoff floor — so skew in the other direction left a message due on the server while the dispatcher still believed it was in the future, unclaimable until the dispatcher caught up. Every predicate now reads the database clock at one resolution. **No action is required**; the delivery guarantee is unchanged, and nothing is delivered earlier than its backoff allows.
- **The Elasticsearch outbox no longer hands the same messages to every poller, and a late failure report can no longer resurrect a delivered message.** Three defects in the same store, all reachable from the last published release.
- **The documented Postgres and Oracle outbox connection-string paths could not start.** Both registered their database dependency with a no-argument factory lambda, which cannot bind the factory overload and bound the instance overload instead — registering a `Func<NpgsqlConnection>` (and `Func<OracleConnection>`) under its own type. `IDb`, which the store actually resolves, was registered nowhere in the framework, so `AddOutbox(o => o.UsePostgres(connectionString))` produced a store that threw `No service for type Excalibur.Data.IDb has been registered` the first time anything resolved it. Both now register `IDb` explicitly; the builder paths that supply a data source were unaffected.
- **Leader election gates the outbox drain on every registration path, not most of them.** The generic path and three store-specific verbs wired the gate; the builder and factory-only paths did not — Consul, Kubernetes and in-memory among them — so a host that reached leader election through one of those verbs got an outbox draining **on every instance at once**, which is the failure leader election is registered to prevent. `AddPostgresLeaderElection`, `AddPostgresHealthBasedLeaderElection`, `AddSqlServerHealthBasedLeaderElection` and the two Consul per-resource registrations made an election resolvable but never registered the outbox leader gate, so nothing carried the fencing token to the drain and a superseded leader could claim and complete messages it no longer owned. All paths now register the gate. **If you register leader election through a builder rather than the generic `AddLeaderElection(...)` entry point, you were exposed to concurrent drain across your instances and no longer are.** Handlers must still be idempotent.
- **In-memory leader election could leave a stopped process holding leadership forever, and a leader relinquishing could delete its successor's record.** Acquiring leadership checked that the candidate was running and then took the resource — two steps, with a shutdown able to land between them — and a lease-renewal callback already dispatched to the thread pool keeps running after its timer is disarmed, so both paths could take the resource *after* the shutdown had run its release and found nothing to release, locking every other instance out. The shutdown, dispose and unhealthy step-down paths could likewise delete a record a successor had just written, leaving that successor believing it still led while the resource stood free.
- **A handler could run one more time than it should, and the inbox would then report that message as never processed at all.** This affects the in-memory, MongoDB, SQL Server, PostgreSQL, Oracle, Cosmos DB, DynamoDB and Elasticsearch inbox stores. Two related races underlie it: the in-memory store told two callers at once that each of them held the same message, and the second one's write erased the first one's record; and on MongoDB, a plain claim stores no lease expiry, and the lease path's "has this lease expired?" test read that stored-but-empty value as a date infinitely far in the past — because an empty value sorts below every real date — so a message that had just been claimed by one caller read to the other as a long-abandoned claim and was handed straight to it. Both callers were told `true`, both ran the handler, the message produced two sets of side effects, and nothing reported an error. The in-memory store backs `AddInMemoryInboxStore()` and is what the local-development and testing paths run on.
- **A MongoDB inbox entry whose processing host died was never retried, and the message was lost silently.** The atomic lease claim inserted the entry without a retry-count field, and the drain that re-admits abandoned and failed entries selects on `retryCount < maxRetries` -- a range comparison, which in MongoDB does not match a document where the field is absent. A lease-created entry was therefore invisible to that read permanently: once the host holding the lease died, no processor held the message and nothing would ever select it again. There was no error and no dead-letter entry; the message simply stopped existing as far as the inbox was concerned. The relational providers never showed this, because their column carries a `NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` and the field is always present. The claim now writes the retry count explicitly on insert.
  **This defect is present in `10.0.0-alpha.8` and affects only `Excalibur.Inbox.MongoDB`.** Moving to a later pre-release stops new entries being stranded, but it does **not** repair entries already written by the affected version -- those documents still lack the field and stay invisible to the retry drain. If you have run the MongoDB inbox on that version, treat any message whose consumer crashed mid-handling as unprocessed: audit the collection for entries left in `Processing` with no retry count, set that field to `0` so the drain can see them, or re-drive the affected messages from their source.
- **Composite deduplication keys were not injective across six inbox and change-data-capture stores**, so two different message identities could produce the same key and a message could be treated as an already-seen duplicate and silently dropped. The in-memory deduplicator used by light-mode inbox processing and by idempotent handlers could also hand the same message id to two callers at once, because a caller that removed an expired entry removed whatever occupied that id rather than the entry it had read.
- **`Excalibur.Inbox.Oracle`: `OracleInboxStore` now implements `IScopedTransactionalInboxStore`.** It reported `SupportsTransactional = true` while lacking the seam the inbox middleware type-tests to select the exactly-once path, so an undecorated Oracle inbox silently processed at-least-once instead. Handlers must still be idempotent across a process crash; what changes is that the handler's writes and the processed-mark now commit in one Oracle transaction on the success path.
- **The inbox carries the logical handler type in its own field** rather than parsing it back out of the deduplication id, so the identifier's format is no longer load-bearing for handler resolution.
- **A dead-lettered message drained in a batch was filed under no tenant.** The dead-letter queue stores an entry under the ambient tenant, so whoever files it has to establish that tenant. The single-message path did, by wrapping its whole body in a tenant scope; the batch path opened its scope inside the parallel worker, deferred failures to a list, and drained that list afterwards — by which time the scope was gone — so a dead letter belonging to one tenant was recorded under the untenanted sentinel. It failed silently, because the destination column is not nullable and the sentinel satisfies it, so the entry simply became invisible to the tenant it came from. The tenant is now established where the entry is filed, from the message itself. Single-message routing is unchanged.
- **The Postgres dead-letter store can list messages.** `GetMessagesAsync` built its filter clauses by appending space-prefixed fragments, then appended the trailing `ORDER BY`/`LIMIT`/`OFFSET` from a raw string literal that began at column zero — so the statement ran as `... = @TenantIdORDER BY ...` and the store **threw on every call**, including against an empty store, with or without filters. Listing dead-lettered messages on Postgres was unavailable rather than degraded. **SQL Server was never affected.**

#### Event sourcing, sagas and projections

- **Optimistic-concurrency losers were misreported by four event stores.** SQL Server reported some losers of a concurrent append as an ordinary failure rather than a concurrency conflict, because its unique-constraint handler matched on the driver's exception type while the insert runs through the data-request seam, which wraps that exception — so a reload-and-retry policy keyed on `IsConcurrencyConflict` never fired and `IEventSourcedRepository` raised a resource error where it should have raised a concurrency error. The handler now unwraps before classifying, on both the plain and outbox-staging append paths. The PostgreSQL, Cosmos DB and SQLite stores misreported the same thing, one of them in the opposite direction: a version reported in conflict with itself. In every case no events were lost or written twice and exactly one writer won every race — what was wrong is what the loser was told.
