- **BREAKING — SQLite snapshots survive the upgrade instead of breaking every read and write.** An existing SQLite snapshot database was created before the snapshot table had a tenant column, and the store creates its table with `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, which does not alter a table that already exists — so the table kept its old shape while the store issued SQL naming a column that was not there, and **every** snapshot read and **every** snapshot write failed with `SQLite Error 1: 'no such column: TenantId'`. Not a degradation, a hard failure on the first snapshot operation after upgrading, and reachable from the last published release: no released version has ever had that column, so any SQLite database in the field is in exactly this state.
- **Right-to-erasure never worked on SQL Server, and erasing a data subject stopped your live subscriptions and projections permanently.** `EraseEventsAsync` tombstones an event by setting its payload to NULL while keeping the row, so the stream's versions stay contiguous and replay does not see a hole — but `001_CreateEventStoreSchema.sql` declared `EventData` as `VARBINARY(MAX) NOT NULL`, so the engine rejected that UPDATE outright: the erase call failed and **no payload was destroyed** — not a partial erase, no erase at all, on the path used to satisfy a data subject's erasure request. Only SQL Server was affected; the Postgres, Oracle, MongoDB and in-memory stores already tombstone this way, and the documented schema on the docs site has always shown the column nullable. Separately, tombstoning replaces an event's type with a reserved marker while preserving its position in the stream; aggregate rehydration and projection rebuilds recognised that marker, but seven other readers did not and instead tried to resolve it as though it were one of your event types.
- **Enabling a decorator silently downgraded the event store's capabilities, and enabling tenancy or projection encryption made pagination disappear.** With the transactional outbox strategy the repository checked `_eventStore is ITransactionalEventStore` to decide whether events and outbox rows could be written in one transaction — but every event store decorator (telemetry, throughput metrics, tenant scoping, encryption, tiered storage) declared only `IEventStore`, so enabling any one of them made the check fail and quietly downgraded the write to non-transactional staging on providers that support the atomic path, with nothing logged. `IEventStoreArchive` was dropped the same way. `TenantScopedProjectionStore` and `EncryptingProjectionStoreDecorator` implemented only `IProjectionStore<T>`, so the `store is IPageableProjectionStore<T>` check the documentation told you to write returned `false` once either decorator was in place, against providers that implement pagination perfectly well — offset pagination is implemented by three stores and cursor pagination by two, and all of it became undetectable the moment tenancy was enabled, silently demoting callers to whole-set queries.
- **`SagaTimeoutDeliveryService` requires the saga type registry it was already depending on.** The parameter was optional and defaulted to null, but it is the only type-resolution path in the service — so a host that composed the service without one resolved nothing and **retired every due timeout undelivered**, silently. It is now a required constructor parameter that throws if absent; the framework's own registration already supplies it.
- **Four Firestore snapshot and write defects.** Two tenants could share one snapshot document, because snapshot ids join tenant, type and aggregate id with `_` and escaped each part but not the separator — so tenant `a` with type `b_c` and tenant `a_b` with type `c` produced the same id, and one tenant could read and overwrite the other's snapshot. The separator is now escaped along with `%` and `/`, making the id injective. **Snapshots stored under an aggregate type or id containing `_` are written at a new id and the old document is not read again**; no data is lost, because a snapshot is an optimisation over the event stream, but the first load of an affected aggregate replays instead of restoring. A snapshot prune could also delete a newer snapshot than the one it checked, the version test and the delete being separate operations; both now run in one transaction. And Firestore re-runs a transaction callback when the transaction conflicts, so three call sites that recorded their verdict in a variable set inside that callback without clearing it per attempt let a conflict, missing document or etag mismatch outlive the attempt that observed it — the retry committed and the caller was told the write had failed, which for the event store meant a successful append raising a concurrency error and prompting callers to append again.
- **Concurrent snapshot saves to Firestore no longer stall on a document lock, and a concurrency guarantee that was never being checked now is.** `SaveSnapshotAsync` arbitrated concurrent savers with a Firestore transaction, which takes a lock on the document; ten savers contending for one aggregate's snapshot could leave *every* one of them waiting and none of them landing, for minutes at a time, and no attempt budget small enough to be sane in production was large enough to outlast it. The save now uses a conditional write — read the document, then create it if it was absent or update it under the update time the read observed, and re-read if the server rejects the write — so nothing is locked and a losing writer is told immediately rather than queued. The stored document is unchanged and needs no migration; `MaxContendedWriteAttempts` now defaults to **16** rather than 40, because without a lock to outlast a writer needs at most one extra attempt per concurrent writer holding a lower version, and across 20 runs of ten concurrent savers the deepest any writer reached was attempt 5. If you had raised this option to compensate for the old behaviour, you can lower it again. The conformance arm covering concurrent rising versions had been shadowed by a weaker local test, so the suite read as complete while the shipped contract went unexercised; the arm is now wired, and it passes.
- **The SQLite event store resolves again through the keyed registration.** Removing the persistence provider factory also removed a keyed `IEventStore` registration that resolution by provider name depends on; it is now registered by the provider's own path rather than by a factory.

#### Change data capture

- **A resumed DynamoDB consumer skipped records whenever a shard split.** The iterator for a shard with no saved position was chosen by asking whether the current position was "the beginning" — but that stops being true after the first record any consumer handles, so on every consumer that had ever made progress the safe branch was unreachable and each newly discovered shard opened at `LATEST`. An unknown shard is almost always a split child, and DynamoDB gives a split child every write since the split, so those writes were skipped silently: no exception, no gap counter, no log line, while the processor appeared to make healthy progress. A shard with a saved sequence number still resumes immediately after it; every other shard now reads from the trim horizon, and starting from "now" is honoured only where it is unambiguous. **Expect redelivery after a split rather than a gap; handlers must be idempotent, which at-least-once delivery already required.**
- **SQL Server change-data-capture recovery from a stale position gave up on the first transient database fault**, stopping the capture producer for the rest of the run, whenever a recovery strategy other than `Throw` is configured. Recovery settings were also silently discarded: the registration installed a pre-built options instance rather than routing the type through the options pipeline, so a consumer's `Configure<CdcRecoveryOptions>(...)` and anything bound from `appsettings` were never applied and the recovery strategy, retry attempt count and retry delay always took their built-in defaults, with no error and no warning. **If you configured these settings and observed the default behavior anyway, this is why**; on upgrade your configured values take effect for the first time, which may change recovery timing in a system tuned around the defaults.
- **A change-data-capture position token changed every time it was rebuilt.** The DynamoDB and Firestore position tokens included the wall-clock instant at which the object was constructed, so two tokens for the same place in the stream never matched — tokens could not be compared, deduplicated or diffed, and a reloaded position never equalled the position it was saved from. This also disagreed with the types' own equality, which had always ignored that instant. Tokens are now a function of the position alone.
- **A change-data-capture processor disposed a Cosmos DB client it did not own**, leaving an already-constructed client disposed underneath the code that supplied it.

#### Data providers

- **An Oracle migration script that refused to run still reported success, so an unattended pipeline recorded a declined migration as applied.** The shipped Oracle upgrade and migration scripts signal a refusal -- a tenant identifier too long to narrow, a table that is not the shape the script expects -- by raising an error from a PL/SQL block. SQL*Plus returns exit code 0 for that unless the script asks it not to, so a runner that checks the exit code saw success, moved on, and applied the next step to a database that had not been changed. Every Oracle script that can refuse now carries `WHENEVER SQLERROR EXIT FAILURE ROLLBACK`, so a refusal is a non-zero exit; a successful run is unaffected and still exits 0. The PostgreSQL scripts already behaved this way. If you run these interactively, note that a failure now ends the SQL*Plus session -- issue `WHENEVER SQLERROR CONTINUE` beforehand if you would rather keep it.
- **A batch write to Firestore reported success for a write it never performed, and writing any document failed once the write could be attempted.** `ExecuteBatchAsync` decided what to do with a create or a replace by testing the operation against an interface that no type in the framework implemented and that no consumer could implement, because it was not public — so the test could never succeed, an unmatched operation was skipped, the batch committed without it, and every operation in the batch was still reported back as succeeded with status 200. Nothing was logged and nothing threw, so a caller learned about the missing document whenever they next went looking for it. Only deletes ever reached Firestore. Underneath that, the provider converted a document to the map Firestore stores by round-tripping it through JSON into a dictionary, which leaves every value as a `JsonElement` — a type the Firestore SDK has no converter for — so any document with a field raised *"Unable to create converter for type System.Text.Json.JsonElement"*, affecting `CreateAsync` and `UpdateAsync` as well as the batch. This had gone unseen because the provider's conformance suite covers metadata, capabilities and health but never wrote a document; the single-document round trip is now covered against a real Firestore emulator alongside the batch.
- **Configuring a Firestore emulator host threw at startup in four packages, and two stores configured for different emulators could talk to the same one.** `Excalibur.Data.Firestore`, `Excalibur.EventSourcing.Firestore`, `Excalibur.Inbox.Firestore` and `Excalibur.Saga.Firestore` built their client with both an explicit endpoint and `EmulatorDetection.EmulatorOnly`, which the client library rejects, so any registration supplying an emulator host failed to resolve a client at all; they now pass the endpoint with insecure credentials, which is the combination that reaches an emulator. The stores also routed their emulator host through the process-wide `FIRESTORE_EMULATOR_HOST` variable, which is first-write-wins, and the helper reports a conflicting value by returning `false` while every caller discarded it — so the second store silently used the first store's endpoint and kept using it after that endpoint was gone. Every Firestore store now takes its endpoint per instance and no longer writes that variable.
- **The Elasticsearch and OpenSearch providers reported themselves available without ever contacting the cluster.** `InitializeAsync` on both performed no I/O at all — it set a flag and returned — so `IsAvailable` was `true` against a cluster that was unreachable, misconfigured or not running, and health reporting built on it was answering from a value it had not verified. Both now verify reachability, the same ping behind `TestConnectionAsync`, before reporting available, and throw `InvalidOperationException` when the cluster does not answer, matching the MySQL provider's long-standing behavior. **If initialization now throws where it previously succeeded, the cluster was already unreachable and calls against it were already failing; the failure has moved to startup, where it is diagnosable.**
- **A disposed SQL Server persistence provider reported itself available.** `IsAvailable` on that provider is latched by its health probe rather than derived from disposal, and `DisposeCoreAsync` never cleared it, so after disposal it kept reporting the last successful probe and a health check or failover routine would keep selecting a provider whose connection semaphore had already been released. Every other provider folds disposal into that expression.
- **The Redis persistence provider reported that it supported transactions, then threw on every use.** `GetService(typeof(IPersistenceProviderTransaction))` returned the provider, and every method on that capability throws — Redis has no client-side transaction, and the atomicity it offers is server-side Lua — so asking whether the capability was available answered yes and the refusal arrived later as a `NotSupportedException` from something you had been told you had. It now returns `null`, the documented way to decline, so you can branch on the answer at discovery. The health capability is genuinely implemented and is still offered.
- **The OpenSearch projection store ignored a client you registered and talked to the default local node instead.** `AddOpenSearchProjectionStore` built a client from `OpenSearchProjectionStoreOptions.NodeUri` unconditionally, so a consumer who deliberately registered their own `IOpenSearchClient` — configured for their cluster, their credentials, their transport settings — got a store that quietly read and wrote somewhere else. The registration now prefers a registered `IOpenSearchClient` and falls back to `NodeUri` only when none is present.
- **A host that configured Elasticsearch security through the documented entry point could not start.** `AddElasticsearchSecurity(configuration, configureOptions)` registers the security monitoring background service, and the monitor it resolves asked for its settings as a bare `SecurityMonitoringOptions`, which nothing registered, so startup failed with *Unable to resolve service for type 'SecurityMonitoringOptions'*. The registration that would have supplied it had been removed on the grounds that init-only properties cannot be set — true of a `Configure` delegate, but not of the configuration binder, which assigns by reflection and is what every neighbouring settings type in the package already uses. `AddElasticsearchSecurity` also now registers an `IHttpClientFactory`: the Elasticsearch authentication provider takes one for OAuth2 token operations and nothing in the package supplied it, so resolving `IElasticsearchAuthenticationProvider` failed unless the host had called `AddHttpClient()` itself. `Microsoft.Extensions.Http` is now a direct dependency of the package.
- **Three relational retry policies grew their backoff without a ceiling**, so a configuration the validator accepted could sleep for hours inside a single data request. This affects the Postgres, MySQL and SQL Server data providers.
- **The SQL Server migrator could never acquire its own lock, so no migration ever ran, and both migrators recorded a checksum they never compared.** `SqlServerMigrator` took its serialization lock with `sp_getapplock` and did not name a lock owner, so the procedure defaulted to transaction ownership while the migrator calls it on a bare connection with no transaction open — SQL Server rejects that outright, returning `-999` rather than blocking, and the migrator read any negative value as contention. Every call to `MigrateAsync` and `RollbackAsync` therefore returned *"Failed to acquire migration lock. Another migration may be in progress"* on every database, with no second instance anywhere, and `SqlServerMigrationHostedService` turned that into a failure to start. The lock is now taken and released with session ownership: held for the whole run while each migration commits in its own transaction, and released when the connection closes even if the process dies mid-run. A rejected acquisition reports the code the server returned, so a genuine wait-timeout (`-1`) reads differently from a rejected call. `PostgresMigrator` was never affected, because Postgres advisory locks are session-scoped already. Both migrators also hashed each migration script into the history table's `Checksum` column and read it back with no code path comparing it — whether a migration was pending was decided by its id alone, so a database that had run an earlier body of a numbered migration carried a row asserting the migration was applied, a stored hash proving it was a different script, and a migrator that never looked; later migrations were applied on top of a schema nobody had verified.
- **`SqlServerPersistenceProvider`'s constructor no longer takes a `SqlDataAccessPolicyFactory`.** The dependency was required by DI and never read. Nothing to do if you register through `AddSqlServerPersistence`; if you construct the provider yourself, drop the last argument.

#### Configuration that never took effect

- **Options types across fifteen packages advertised startup validation that never ran.** Each declared an `IValidateOptions<T>` stating the rules its options must satisfy, and each registration path called `ValidateOnStart()`, but the validator was never added to the container — so `ValidateOnStart()` resolved the options and validated nothing, and a host configured with a missing connection string, a non-positive timeout or a batch size outside its permitted range started cleanly and failed later, at the first operation that depended on the value, with an error describing the symptom rather than the configuration that caused it. The validators are now registered by the `Add*` method of the package that owns them. This affects Change Data Capture for Cosmos DB, DynamoDB, SQL Server and in-memory; the Cosmos DB and Postgres projection stores; the Firestore snapshot store; materialized views; snapshot upgrading; and the dispatch timeout middleware. **Behavior change on upgrade:** a host whose configuration was already invalid now fails at startup instead of at first use. That is the intended outcome, but it surfaces at a different moment than before; no shipped default violates any of these rules, so a host that starts today on default configuration continues to. Two further options types were validated by rules bound to a base class nothing resolves — `ProviderOptions` (shared transport settings) and the adaptive-TTL cache options — so their rules were unreachable regardless of registration; the provider timeout and retry-policy delay rules now run for the AWS and Azure transports, and the adaptive TTL bounds for the rule-based cache. One error message is corrected with them: an invalid provider timeout used to advise configuring `ProviderOptions` directly, which has no effect, and now points at the transport's own provider options.
- **Named transports overwrote each other's options.** A named registration configured the same *unnamed* options instance as every other registration of that transport, so whichever ran last silently supplied the settings for **both** — two SQS transports named for different queues ran against one queue, a per-transport KMS key could not reach its own queue, and a second `AddAzureServiceBusTransport(...)`, `AddKafkaTransport("...", ...)` or `AddRabbitMQTransport("...", ...)` in one container replaced the first one's entity, brokers, consumer group, queue and prefetch settings. It failed silently rather than throwing, and the shipped XML examples demonstrated exactly the broken configuration — our own published example for `AddAzureServiceBusTransport` shows the two-transport scenario. Ten transports were affected: AWS SQS, AWS SNS, AWS EventBridge, Azure Service Bus, Azure Event Hubs, Azure Storage Queue, Google Pub/Sub, Kafka, RabbitMQ and gRPC. Each named registration now configures and resolves its own options instance; the unnamed instance is still configured, so types taking `IOptions<KafkaOptions>` or `IOptions<RabbitMqOptions>` directly are unaffected, and **a host with a single transport of any of these types sees no change at all.** If you worked around this by splitting hosts, or by keeping two configurations deliberately identical, you can stop.
- **Six audit exporters and one audit sink ignored the HTTP client they were configured with.** The Elasticsearch, OpenSearch, AWS CloudWatch, Datadog, Google Cloud Logging, Microsoft Sentinel and Splunk audit packages each registered a typed `HttpClient` with `AddHttpClient` — applying the timeout, the resilience pipeline, the node-failover handler and, for Splunk, the primary handler that honours `ValidateCertificate` — and then registered the exporter a second time by its implementation type. That second registration is what a consumer resolves, and the container builds it from its own plain `HttpClient` rather than the configured one. Everything applied to the typed client was therefore inert on the only path anything uses: `MaxRetryAttempts`, `RetryBaseDelay` and `Timeout` had no effect, every node past the first in `NodeUrls` was never contacted, requests used the 100-second default timeout, and Splunk's certificate-validation setting was not applied. The exporters and the sink now resolve through the typed client. This was measured against a real container before and after — the resolved `HttpClient` was a different instance carrying the default timeout — rather than inferred from the registration code.
- **`ClaimCheckOptions.EnableMetrics` now controls whether claim check metrics are emitted.** The property defaulted to `true` and no provider read it, so it neither switched anything on nor off: no claim check counters, duration histograms or payload-size histograms were produced regardless of what you set. `AddClaimCheck<TProvider>(...)` now wraps your provider in the instrumentation decorator when the option is set, and registers the provider undecorated when it is not. Meter and activity-source name: `Excalibur.Dispatch.Patterns.ClaimCheck`. Metrics are on by default and will begin appearing where they previously did not; if you resolve `IClaimCheckProvider` and cast it to your own provider type, that cast now fails while metrics are enabled — depend on the interface, or set `EnableMetrics = false`.
- **A Pub/Sub batch aimed every message it held at your handler at once, ignoring the concurrency you configured, and the pull receiver ignored its request timeout.** `BatchOptions.ConcurrentBatchProcessors` bounded how many batches were received concurrently but never reached the fan-out *inside* a batch, which started one task per message and awaited them together. A batch holds up to `MaxMessagesPerBatch`, which defaults to 1000, so a consumer who set that option to protect a downstream service still saw up to a thousand concurrent handler invocations against it — on their infrastructure, not ours. The processor now fans out under `Parallel.ForEachAsync` with `MaxDegreeOfParallelism` set from the option, so the peak number of in-flight handler invocations is the number you asked for, and a cancelled batch surfaces `OperationCanceledException` as the ordering-aware processor already did. `PubSubTransportReceiver` also accepted a request timeout and never applied it.
- **A sharded host with no ambient tenant was routed to the default shard instead of being refused.** `ITenantShardMap.CurrentShard(ITenantContext)` and `IAmbientTenantStoreResolver<TStore>.ResolveCurrent()` substituted an empty tenant identifier when the tenant context had resolved no tenant, and a shard map answers an unrecognised identifier with the shard named by `ShardMapOptions.DefaultShardId` — so with a default shard configured the call succeeded and handed back that shard's store, and the caller read and wrote whichever tenant that shard holds, with no exception, no log, and no other signal. With no default shard configured the empty identifier reached `TenantShardNotFoundException`, a confusing report of the same fault. The ambient tenant scope is also now established where the tenant is actually read, rather than at a point some read paths did not pass through.

#### Auditing and compliance

- **The audit trail never named who acted — every record reported the actor as "System" — and `AddAuditContext()` documented a middleware it never registered.** `IActivityContext.AccessToken()` read a context entry that nothing in the framework ever wrote, so `ActivityAudit` fell back to its no-caller value on every record: `Login`, `UserId` and `UserName` all read "System", and the `X-Raised-By` header on a record that took the outbox fallback read "Unknown" — including for an action dispatched in a request scope carrying a live `IAccessToken`. An audit trail that cannot name the actor does not satisfy the obligations it is kept for. The audit middleware now takes the caller's access token from the same scope it takes the activity context from. Separately, `AddAuditContext()` promised the scoped `IAuditContext` was filled with the message's correlation id, tenant and actor before a handler runs and registered nothing to fill it, so every entry a handler recorded through an injected `IAuditContext` carried no correlation id, no tenant and an actor of "unknown" while the API documenting it said the opposite; it now registers that middleware and resolves the audit context and the `IAuditActorProvider` from the scope each message is processed in. An action dispatched without a request scope has no caller and is still recorded as "System", as is an anonymous caller.
- **Every audit record after the first carried the first caller's tenant.** `AuditMiddleware` took the scoped `IActivityContext` as a constructor dependency, but a dispatch middleware instance is built once from the root provider and lives for the process — so the tenant, correlation id, client address and ETag captured for the first auditable action were written into every audit record the process produced afterwards, across tenants. On a multi-tenant host the audit trail attributed one tenant's activity to another. The middleware now resolves the audit context from the scope each action is dispatched in.
- **RBAC audit stores answered with one caller's identity and role for the life of the process, and could not start at all under scope validation.** `AddRbacAuditStore` and `AddAuditAnnotations` register their decorators with a singleton lifetime and took the role provider, the actor provider and the meta-audit logger as constructor arguments — all per-caller services — so the container resolved them once, from the root, and the same values answered every later call. Every meta-audit entry, the record of who read or annotated the audit trail, named whichever caller happened to be first; the role check, which is an access-control decision, was decided on that caller's role; and the annotation stores stamped that caller's identity onto each annotation as its author, which also decides which annotations a later read returns. With scope validation enabled — the default in an ASP.NET Core development environment — the wiring the documentation describes did not start, failing with "cannot consume scoped service from singleton". The stores now open a scope per operation, so none of these can be held across callers.
- **Audit integrity verification was wrong in four ways, in both directions.** An intact multi-tenant trail was reported as tampered, because the SQL Server and Postgres stores write one hash chain per tenant and application while verification compared each record against whichever record happened to sit next to it in the global write order — so every estate with more than one tenant or application saw `ViolationsDetected` on a trail nothing had touched, and a verifier that reports tampering on healthy data is one that gets switched off, taking the real detections with it. Untenanted records failed their own verification, because the integrity tag is computed over the record as supplied, where an untenanted event carries no tenant, while the database column holds a reserved term instead — so verification re-computed the tag over a different record than the one signed; the stored term is now folded back to the value that was signed, with **no migration and no re-tagging**, because the tags were always correct. In the other direction, a record deleted from the *middle* of a trail reported as intact, because each record was checked against its own stored claim about its predecessor, which lives in the same row and survives the removal of the record it names; and a date-ranged verification could not detect records deleted from the *front* of a range, because the first record in range was treated as though it began the chain. Each record is now checked against the record that actually precedes it, the walk is seeded with the tag of the record immediately preceding the range, and a verified range examines the contiguous run of the chain spanning it — so a record written between two others but stamped outside the range no longer leaves a hole indistinguishable from a deletion, and `EventsVerified` counts what was examined, which can exceed the number of records whose timestamps fall inside the window. Asserting that a range starts at the beginning of the chain is now something a caller states explicitly rather than something verification assumes.
- **BREAKING — audit integrity verification reports three outcomes, and `AuditIntegrityResult.IsValid` is removed.** Verification could not distinguish *"I examined 4,000 events and every hash held"* from *"I examined nothing"* — both were `IsValid == true`. Every audit store returned that same passing result for a window containing no events, and the SOC2 audit-logging control turned it into an evidence record reading **"Audit log integrity verification: Passed"** for a period in which no record was read at all. That is an assurance nobody earned, in a document handed to an external auditor.
- **BREAKING — an encrypted audit query is refused instead of answered with an empty result set.** Audit field encryption is randomized, so two records holding the same value hold different ciphertext and no comparison against the plaintext you supply can match either. A query filtering on such a field previously returned zero rows, which reads as *"no such events"* while the events are present — the worst answer available from a compliance trail. It now throws `NotSupportedException` naming the field and the option that governs it.
- **BREAKING — an erasure store's failures can be told apart by the caller, and a duplicate legal hold no longer looks like an unresolved tenant.** `IErasureStore` specified `InvalidOperationException` as meaning exactly *"a request with this RequestId already exists"* and invited you to branch on it, but **four** conditions raised that type: the duplicate, an absent or stale schema, a missing-tenant failure, and a disposed store. Only the first means the request is safely stored; on the other three nothing was written, so a caller reading *"already on file"* stopped retrying and an erasure request was dropped with nothing anywhere reporting it. `DuplicateErasureRequestException` and `DuplicateErasureCertificateException` now signal the genuine duplicate, and schema and disposal failures surface as themselves. `ILegalHoldStore.SaveHoldAsync` likewise raises the new **`DuplicateLegalHoldException`** on the in-memory, PostgreSQL and SQL Server stores instead of a bare `InvalidOperationException` — `TenantRequiredException` also derives from `InvalidOperationException`, so the natural `catch (InvalidOperationException) { /* already filed */ }` treated a hold that was **never written** as one already on file, and a legal hold blocks erasure, so losing one does not fail safe: the next erasure runs and reports success over data you were required to keep. All the new types derive from `InvalidOperationException`, so existing broad catches still compile; narrow them.
- **An expiring access review campaign logged that reviewers had been notified when nobody had, and never extended the deadline it said it extended.** The `NotifyAndExtend` expiry policy resolved a notifier that fell back to a no-op default when you had registered none, called it, and then wrote `Campaign '...' extended and reviewers notified.` to the log — an access-review audit trail asserting a notification that was never sent. The same branch then marked the campaign **Expired** rather than extending it, so `ExtensionDays` had no effect anywhere despite being documented as the number of days the deadline is extended. Both halves of that log line were false. `NotifyAndExtend` now notifies, then moves `ExpiresAt` forward by `ExtensionDays` from the moment of extension and leaves the campaign **InProgress**, and the log records the new deadline. If no `IAccessReviewNotifier` is registered, or if delivery throws, the campaign is left exactly as it was and the failure is logged as an error on every sweep until you fix it. **Action:** if you use `NotifyAndExtend`, register an `IAccessReviewNotifier`; if you relied on that policy expiring campaigns, use `DoNothing`, which does exactly that and always has.
- **Key escrow shipped with no way to create its tables. If you enabled key escrow before this version, the tables were never created — check whether any escrow actually persisted before relying on it for recovery.** The SQL Server escrow service writes to `KeyEscrow`, `RecoveryTokens` and `KeyEscrowWrap`, and nothing in any published package could create them: no script, no schema authored in code, no auto-create path, and nothing in the README to copy. The feature was registered and wired, so registration succeeded and the failure arrived later, at the first escrow write, as `Invalid object name`.
- **A FIPS-enabled Windows host was reported as not FIPS compliant, always. If you recorded that result in a compliance artefact, it was a false negative.** The detector read `CryptoConfig.AllowOnlyFipsAlgorithms` to decide whether a Windows host was in FIPS mode, but on .NET Core and later that property is hardcoded to `false` and is never populated from the host policy — so the answer was `false` on every Windows host, including one genuinely running under FIPS policy. Detection now reads the `FipsAlgorithmPolicy` value under `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa`.

#### Claim check, buffers and dispatch internals

- **The Azure Blob claim-check provider returned a reference with an empty `BlobName`, and threw the wrong exception for a missing payload.** The blob name was computed, used, and then dropped, so a reference you persisted could not be used to locate the payload out of band. A missing claim surfaced as `InvalidOperationException` while the in-memory, S3 and Google Cloud Storage providers all throw `KeyNotFoundException` — which is also what our own documentation hands you as the `catch`. **If you followed that example, your handler never fired and you got an unhandled exception on exactly the recovery path you wrote it for.**
- **`DeleteAsync` on the S3 claim-check store and the DynamoDB change-data-capture checkpoint delete both reported success whether or not anything was there.** Each contract defines the return value as an observation — "true if it was deleted, false if it did not exist" — and each implementation returned a constant, so the value carried no information. Both services make their delete deliberately idempotent and silent about what they removed, which is exactly why the answer has to be requested: the checkpoint delete now asks DynamoDB for the prior item, and the claim-check delete checks for the object first. **If you built cleanup reconciliation or a "did I actually free that?" audit on either return value, it was previously always affirmative.** The Firestore checkpoint delete stopped claiming success for the same reason.
- **Pooled buffers could be returned to the wrong pool, or returned twice.** Three distinct defects in buffer ownership: a buffer constructed with a caller-supplied pool rented from that pool but returned to a different one, and two types guarded disposal with a check-then-act flag that could not prevent a double return under concurrent disposal. Ownership is now single and disposal is atomic. A double return corrupts a pool for every later renter, so the symptom appears far from the cause.
- **Building a service provider on one thread while another froze the handler cache could throw `NullReferenceException`.** `HandlerInvokerRegistry` tested a `_isFrozen` flag and then dereferenced `_warmupCache`, which `FreezeCache` sets to `null` immediately after raising that flag, so a reader that observed the flag as `false` and lost the race dereferenced a field that had just been cleared — and the null-forgiving operators on those four sites suppressed the warning that would have shown it. The registry now reads each field once and tests the frozen cache directly: it is published before the flag is raised and before the warmup cache is cleared, so a reader that sees it has a complete cache and one that does not still has the warmup cache. Surfaced during handler pre-warm inside `AddDispatchHandlers`, so it was reachable from ordinary registration.
- **Dispatch under Native AOT could build a result reflectively, which succeeded or failed depending on the result type.** The plain dispatch path — no routing, validation or authorization result, which is the common case — resolved its result factory through `MakeGenericType` and a compiled expression tree *before* the AOT-safe registry was consulted, so the registry was unreachable on the hot path. A reference-type result often survived through shared generics while a value-type result that had not been statically instantiated did not, which made it a per-type coin flip, harder to diagnose than an outright failure.
- **Health-check serialization no longer mutates the shared serializer options.** It added converters to the process-wide instance, which leaked them into every other consumer of those options and threw once that instance had been used, since `JsonSerializerOptions` becomes read-only on first use.
- **`TransportSecurityException` reported `Unspecified` as the reason for every failure and never said which transport raised it.** Both `FailureReason` and `TransportName` existed on the exception and were set at no throw site in the framework, so a consumer branching on the reason took the fallback branch every time, and a host wiring more than one transport could not tell which one had refused. Every throw site now populates both: the TLS refusals raised by the transport connection base, by Kafka and by RabbitMQ report `TlsNotEnabled` and name their transport.

### Removed

- **BREAKING — the persistence provider factory and its configuration surface are removed; resolve a provider from keyed dependency injection instead.** `IPersistenceProviderFactory`, `PersistenceProviderFactory`, `IConfigurableProvider`, `PersistenceProviderType`, `PersistenceProviderOptions`, `IPersistenceConfiguration`, `PersistenceConfiguration`, the `AddPersistence(...)` overloads taking a configuration callback or an `IConfiguration`, `IPersistenceHealthCheck`, `PersistenceHealthStatus` and `DetailedHealthCheckResult` are gone. `IConfigurableProvider.Configure(PersistenceProviderOptions)` stood beside the live entry point all eleven providers implement, `IPersistenceProvider.InitializeAsync`, and **no provider ever implemented it** — so every one of the sixteen required, range-validated settings on `PersistenceProviderOptions` was bound from configuration, validated, logged, and then read by nothing that configures a provider. The factory mapped a configured provider name onto the keyed-DI key its package registers under, and that mapping was wrong for five of the eleven providers: MySQL and OpenSearch resolved only if you named them exactly `"mysql"` and `"opensearch"`, and Cosmos DB, DynamoDB and Firestore registered no keyed entry at all. **Replace `IPersistenceProviderFactory.GetProvider(name)` with `GetRequiredKeyedService<IPersistenceProvider>(key)`, or `[FromKeyedServices(key)]` on a constructor parameter.** The non-keyed `IPersistenceProvider` still resolves and still forwards to `"default"`, so a single-provider host needs no change at all; this was already the only path that worked for every provider, and the one our own samples used. `IPersistenceProviderHealth.GetConnectionPoolStatsAsync` goes with it, because it could not report what its name promised: six of the eleven providers have no connection pool to describe and returned `null` or provider identity, MongoDB returned literal zeros with a comment saying the driver does not expose the figure, Redis multiplexes rather than pools and returned socket timeouts and endpoint names, and the three SQL providers queried `pg_stat_activity`, `sys.dm_exec_connections` and `performance_schema.global_status` — which count **every client of that server, from every process**, not the pool belonging to your application. Three different names for one concept had appeared across the family, and no conformance arm covered the member at all.
- **BREAKING — `ITenantResolver` is removed, and it never resolved a tenant.** `DefaultTenantResolver` and the `TenantContextHolder.TenantIdItemKey` constant go with it. The default implementation read the tenant from a message-context item (`"excalibur.dispatch.tenant-id"`) that nothing in the framework ever wrote, so it returned `TenantContextOptions.DefaultTenantId` for every message — including messages belonging to someone else — and a resolver you registered yourself was never called at all, because no framework code path resolved the interface. **If you registered one, any tenant scoping you believed it was doing was not happening — check the code that depended on it.** Use the ambient scope instead, which is the path the framework itself uses: open a `TenantContextHolder.BeginScope(tenantId)` for the duration of the operation and everything inside it — handlers, stores, decorators — reads the tenant through `ITenantContext`. ASP.NET Core hosts get this from `UseTenantIdMiddleware()`; for a dispatched message the tenant is on the message's identity feature, so `TenantContextHolder.BeginScope(context.GetTenantId())` around your pipeline is the direct replacement. One caution when you wire that up: the tenant is derived from a transport header (`X-Tenant-ID` by default), and the ambient scope selects the partition every tenant-aware store reads, so authorise the tenant before scoping to it rather than trusting an inbound header. See [Multi-Tenancy](https://docs.excalibur-dispatch.dev/docs/multi-tenancy).
- **BREAKING — `IVersionedProjectionStore<T>` and `VersionedProjection<T>` are removed.** The interface offered optimistic concurrency on the projection read path — `GetVersionedAsync` returning a projection with its version, and `UpsertVersionedAsync` rejecting a stale one — and told you to detect support with `if (store is IVersionedProjectionStore<T> versioned)`. **No projection store implemented it**, so that test never matched on any store this framework can hand you, and neither method was reachable.
- **BREAKING — `Excalibur.Dispatch.Transport.GooglePubSub`: the streaming-pull subsystem is removed.** `AddGooglePubSubStreamingPull`, `AddStreamingPullHealthCheck`, `StreamingPullOptions` and the related public option and event-argument types are gone.
- **BREAKING — `Excalibur.Hosting.Serverless` is no longer published.** The package declared no public types at all — its shipped API surface was empty — while its README described it as *"core abstractions and interfaces for serverless hosting integration."* Nothing could be written against it, because there was nothing in it to write against.
- **BREAKING — `Excalibur.Data.Postgres`'s audit store and `AddPostgresDataAuditStore` are removed.** Use `Excalibur.AuditLogging.Postgres` and `AddPostgresAuditStore` instead.
- **BREAKING — `ISubjectKeyManager.DestroyKeyAsync` is removed.** Erasure is owned by the erasure service. The member was a second, unguarded route to key destruction: it destroyed a subject's key directly, without the legal-hold check that `IErasureService`/`IErasureExecutor` perform before any key is destroyed, so a caller could erase a data subject whose records were under an active legal hold. It could not be reached from the erasure path either, because an erasure request retains only the pseudonymized subject identifier, never the raw one this member required.
- **BREAKING — the selective inbox configuration API is removed:** `IInboxConfigurationBuilder`, `IInboxConfigurationProvider`, `IInboxHandlerConfiguration` and `InboxHandlerSettings`. The types were public but unreachable — nothing ever accepted an `IInboxConfigurationBuilder`, there was no `ConfigureInbox()` entry point and no DI registration for the provider, so the idempotency middleware never received one and the documented "fluent configuration takes precedence over `[Idempotent]`" rule could not take effect. **What you must do: nothing.** No consumer could construct or supply these types, so no working code referenced them. Configure per-handler idempotency with `[Idempotent]`, which supports the same retention, in-memory/persistent, message-ID strategy and header-name settings and is read by the middleware on the live path.
- **BREAKING — `AddOpenSearchAuditSink` and `AddElasticsearchAuditSink` are removed, together with `OpenSearchAuditSinkOptions` and `ElasticsearchAuditSinkOptions`.** Both entry points registered an internal writer that implemented no framework contract; nothing in the framework resolved it, and because the type was internal you could not resolve it either — so calling them configured a destination that no audit event could ever reach. **Use `AddOpenSearchAuditExporter` / `AddElasticsearchAuditExporter` instead.** They register `IAuditLogExporter`, which the audit pipeline consumes, and cover the same single-event indexing over the same bulk endpoint with the same node-failover and resilience configuration. The builder options you passed to the sink carry over unchanged.
- **BREAKING — `AwsSnsTransportOptions` and `IAwsSnsTransportBuilder.MapTopic<T>(...)` are gone.** `MapTopic<T>` recorded a message-type-to-topic mapping that no part of the SNS transport ever read — SNS publishing resolves a single configured topic ARN, and per-message-type topic routing was never implemented — and the call appeared in our own published example. If you were relying on it you were already getting a single-topic transport; registering one named SNS transport per topic gives you routing that genuinely works. `AwsSnsTransportOptions` was the parallel options model the registration translated into `AwsSnsOptions`, and the builder now configures `AwsSnsOptions` directly, so `ConfigureOptions(...)` hands you an `AwsSnsOptions`, which exposes everything the removed type did apart from `MapTopic` plus the connection and delivery settings it did not. Change field names to their `AwsSnsOptions` equivalents — `Region` is now `Connection.RegionEndpoint`, `MaxErrorRetry` and `Timeout` are `Connection.MaxErrorRetry` and `Connection.Timeout` — or use the unchanged fluent methods, which handle the reshaping for you.
- **BREAKING — the Azure Service Bus batching and entity-prefix options are gone, along with the parallel `AzureServiceBusTransportOptions` model.** `ConfigureSender(...)` accepted `EnableBatching(...)`, `MaxBatchSizeBytes(...)`, `MaxBatchCount(...)`, `BatchWindow(...)` and `WithConfig(key, value)`, and the transport builder accepted `WithEntityPrefix(...)`. All six were stored and never read: the sender has no accumulate-and-flush path to configure, and no prefix was ever applied to an entity name. `EnableBatching` appeared in our own published example, so following it produced a call that did nothing. **If you were setting them you were already getting unbatched sends and unprefixed names**, so the behaviour you get today is unchanged; only the compile-time promise is. Batch by sending your own `ServiceBusMessageBatch`, and map full entity names with `MapEntity<T>(...)`, which does work.
- **BREAKING — `ElasticsearchOutboxOptions.SentMessageRetentionDays` is removed.** Setting it did nothing: the value was range-checked by the options validator and copied through registration, and no code path ever read it, so a consumer who set it got a validated, silently ignored number and an index that grew without bound — while its XML documentation described it as a working TTL, which made the promise convincing from every angle except the one that matters. **If you set this property, delete the assignment** — your build will otherwise fail, and it is the only change you need, because nothing you had configured was in effect. To actually expire sent entries on Elasticsearch, call the store's cleanup operation from your own host with the cutoff you want; the framework schedules nothing for you. This is a binary-breaking removal, taken deliberately while the API is unfrozen.
- **BREAKING — `ClaimCheckStorageOptions.EnableEncryption` is removed.** The flag was never read by any claim check store, so setting it encrypted nothing while reading as a control over payloads at rest. It also could not be made to work where it lived: encryption needs key material, and the claim check package depends on nothing that supplies any. **Delete the assignment** — it is a compile error now rather than a silent no-op — and encrypt where the key lives. Server-side encryption on the bucket or container is on by default for Azure Blob Storage, Amazon S3 and Google Cloud Storage; for client-side encryption, hand the provider a storage client you configured with a key-encryption key.
- **BREAKING — `AuthorizationEffect.Indeterminate` is removed; the effect is now `Permit` or `Deny`.** Nothing in the framework ever produced it, so no evaluator returned it and no `switch` arm on it ever ran. Its contract was already fail-closed — callers were required to treat it as a denial — but its name invites the opposite reading, which is the wrong way to be wrong about an authorization result. The evaluator contract is unchanged in substance: on an error, a timeout or an ambiguous result an implementation returns `Deny`, never a silent permit. If you wrote an evaluator that returned `Indeterminate`, return `Deny`; if you `switch` on the effect, delete the arm — it was unreachable. `Deny` remains the zero value, so a default-initialized effect still fails closed.
- **BREAKING — `IAccessReviewNotifier` loses two members that nothing ever called, and no longer has a default implementation.** `NotifyCampaignCreatedAsync` and `NotifyDecisionRequiredAsync` were part of the interface you implement, and no code path in the framework invoked either one — implementing them was writing a handler for a notification that would never arrive. `NotifyCampaignExtendedAsync`, the one the expiry service actually calls, is unchanged. The no-op default registration is also gone, because a notifier that silently succeeds cannot be told apart from one that delivered, which is what allowed the audit-trail defect above. **Action:** delete the two members from your implementation. If you resolve `IAccessReviewNotifier` from the container without registering one, you now get `null` rather than a no-op instance — register your own, which is the only configuration that ever actually notified anyone.
- **BREAKING — `LazyReEncryptionMiddleware` and `LazyReEncryptionOptions` are removed.** Neither type was registered by any composition method, so there was no supported way to activate the middleware and no configuration that turned it on. **What you must do: nothing**, unless you constructed and registered the middleware yourself. If you did, keep a copy of the type in your own code — its dependencies, `IEncryptionMigrationService` and `IEncryptionProvider`, remain public. Encryption-version migration itself is unaffected and continues to be available through the compliance migration service.
- **BREAKING — `Excalibur.Outbox`: the `OutboxOptions` and `InboxOptions` preset factories and their fluent builder interfaces are no longer public.** `OutboxOptions.Balanced()`, `.HighThroughput()`, `.HighReliability()`, `.Custom()`, the matching four on `InboxOptions`, and the interfaces they returned (`IOutboxOptionsBuilder`, `IOutboxOptionsCoreBuilder`, `IOutboxOptionsReliabilityBuilder`, `IInboxOptionsBuilder`, `IInboxOptionsCoreBuilder`, `IInboxOptionsAdvancedBuilder`) are now internal.
- **BREAKING — the SQL Server and Oracle saga idempotency requests and `SagaIdempotency.sql` are removed.** They were unreachable: `internal sealed`, no references, no registration entry point, and their provider was deleted in an earlier release. The shipped script created a table nothing wrote to, described in its own header as configurable through an options type that no longer exists — so a consumer who ran it got a permanently empty table and a reasonable belief that saga idempotency was durably backed. It was not. Message de-duplication is unchanged and continues to run through saga state, which is tenant-filtered on load. If you provisioned that table, you can drop it.
- **BREAKING — forty public interfaces that no consumer could reach have been deleted.** Eighteen were public and documented and could neither be handed to the framework nor obtained from it: nothing accepted one as a parameter, returned one, constrained a generic on one, or resolved one at runtime, so implementing any of them changed nothing. A further twenty-two had exactly one occurrence in the entire source tree — their own declaration. Three of those were worse than unused: the documentation instructed you to implement `IAuditSink` to persist audit events and `ITokenValidator` to validate tokens, but nothing in the framework ever resolved either, so an implementation was never called; and the documented `IMessageMiddleware<TMessage>` did not exist in any form. That guidance has been corrected rather than deleted. Three persistence options interfaces go with them — `IPersistenceResilienceOptions`, `IPersistencePoolingOptions` and `IPersistenceObservabilityOptions` existed only to be implemented, and `InMemoryProviderOptions`, `PostgresPersistenceOptions` and `SqlServerPersistenceOptions` no longer declare them. If you configure a provider through its `Add…` method and never cast the options object to one of these three, nothing changes for you: every value they exposed is still settable on the options object or its sub-options group.
- **BREAKING — more than forty public options types the framework never read have been removed.** Every public property on each was settable and unreadable — no shipped code path read a value from any of them, so configuring one changed nothing. They comprise `LoggingOptions`, `MessageRoutingOptions`, `TunedArrayPoolOptions`, `CryptoShreddingOptions` and `EventSourcedSagaOptions`; twelve more of the same shape; and twenty-three further types together with two properties on `CredentialRotationOptions`. Also removed: the five in-memory provider settings `MaxRetryAttempts`, `RetryDelayMilliseconds`, `EnableConnectionPooling`, `MaxPoolSize` and `MinPoolSize`, since the in-memory provider neither pools connections nor retries — it holds objects in a dictionary and uses a no-op retry policy; `DispatchOptions.CrossCutting.Observability`, `.Resilience` and `.Caching` with the `ObservabilityOptions`, `ResilienceOptions` and `CachingOptions` types under `Excalibur.Dispatch.Options.Configuration`, two of which were name-twins of live types in other packages, so binding `Dispatch:Observability:EnableTracing` looked correct and did nothing while an identically-named working option existed elsewhere (configure tracing and metrics through `Excalibur.Dispatch.Observability`, and retries, circuit breakers and timeouts through `Excalibur.Dispatch.Resilience.Polly`; `CrossCutting.Performance` is unaffected); `SecurityOptions.EnableSecurityHeaders` and `CustomHeaders`, which described adding headers to HTTP responses that this package has no response pipeline to add them to, so no header was ever emitted; `ClaimCheckStorageOptions.UseHierarchicalStorage`, `.ColdStorageThreshold` and `.ChunkSize`, since no claim check provider implemented hot/cold tiering or chunked transfer; and `CredentialRotationOptions.MaintenanceWindowOnly` and `.MaxRetries` — `MaintenanceWindowOnly` defaulted to `true`, which made it the more misleading of the two, because leaving it alone looked like choosing the cautious setting while credential rotation actually fired whenever the rotation interval elapsed, at any hour, and there is no maintenance-window concept in the package for it to have honoured. `Enabled`, `RotationInterval` and `WarningThreshold` are unchanged and are read as documented. **If one of these appears in your configuration today, delete it** — removing it changes no behaviour, because nothing observed it in the first place.
- **BREAKING — four transport configuration surfaces with no behaviour behind them are removed.** Each shipped an options type, a validator, a registration method and a worked example telling you how to enable something that did not exist; nothing outside each validator ever read the options. Two duplicated machinery that already works — SQS retry is genuinely wired through the AWS SDK's own retry count, and circuit breaking lives in the resilience package and the dispatch middleware — so they were not missing features but second, inert controls sitting beside working ones, which is worse than a plain duplicate because you cannot tell which is which. The Pub/Sub filter described applying a filter when creating subscriptions, and that package has a deliberate policy of never creating subscriptions. **Delete these calls; the working equivalents are named above.** What made this class dangerous is that the plumbing was correct: registration bound, validation ran, and a bad threshold was rejected — everything except the effect.
- **BREAKING — consistency alerting on the Elasticsearch projection tracker is removed.** `IEventualConsistencyTrackerAdmin.ConfigureConsistencyAlertsAsync` accepted an alert configuration, stored it in a field, and never looked at it again — no threshold was ever compared, no alert was ever raised. `ConsistencyAlertOptions`, `AlertSeverityThreshold` and `ProjectionAlertSeverity` are removed with it. If you called it, delete the call: it configured nothing. Lag data is still available through `GetConsistencyMetricsAsync`, so alerting on it remains something you drive from your own monitoring.
- **BREAKING — four exception types nothing raised are removed.** `CosmosDbStalePositionException`, `DynamoDbStalePositionException`, `PostgresStalePositionException` and `TenantScopeNotSupportedException` were public but never thrown, so no `catch` naming one could ever match. SQL Server (`SqlServerCdcStalePositionException`) and Firestore (`FirestoreStalePositionException`) do raise a stale change-data-capture exception and both derive from `CdcStalePositionException`, so catching that base type covers them; the Cosmos DB, DynamoDB and Postgres change-data-capture paths do not report a stale checkpoint as an exception at all, which is why their subclasses had no throw site. The tenancy exception described a refusal that no longer happens.
- **BREAKING — `TransportSecurityFailureReason.CertificateValidationFailed` and `.ConnectionDowngraded` are removed.** No transport could produce either one. The framework performs no certificate validation of its own — that is the platform's TLS stack, which reports its failures through its own exception types — and it does not detect a mid-connection downgrade, because TLS is verified once when the connection is established. A `catch` filter written against either value therefore never matched, and never could. The remaining values keep their numbers: `Unspecified` is `0` and `TlsNotEnabled` is `1`, with `2` through `5` left permanently unused, so a reason already written to a log or a database still means what it meant.
- **BREAKING — `Excalibur.Data.OpenSearch`'s client-side resilience and monitoring subsystems are removed**, and index management is now registered rather than merely present. Neither subsystem was ever wired.
- **`Excalibur.Hosting.HealthChecks` no longer bundles the health-check dashboard, and with it no longer drags a database layer behind a health endpoint.** The package referenced `AspNetCore.HealthChecks.UI`, which is a dashboard *with persistence*: it depends on `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design`, so five Entity Framework Core packages entered the published dependency closure — across two major versions, 8.0.11 and 10.0.7, in the same graph. Entity Framework Core appeared in the package's own published manifest as a **direct** dependency, and it was the single largest third-party source of trim and ahead-of-time warnings in a package that declares itself ahead-of-time compatible. That contradicted this framework's standing rule that it uses Dapper and raw ADO.NET and takes no ORM dependency anywhere.
- **`Excalibur.Dispatch.Patterns` no longer drags a benchmarking harness into your application.** The package carried a plain `BenchmarkDotNet` reference with no `PrivateAssets`, so a development-time benchmarking tool was a runtime dependency of a shipped package and every consumer of it — directly or transitively — resolved and restored BenchmarkDotNet and its own dependency tree along with it. Nothing in the package's public surface changes; if you were relying on BenchmarkDotNet arriving this way, reference it directly in the project that benchmarks.
- **`Excalibur.Compliance` no longer forces both database drivers onto every consumer.** It declared `MongoDB.Driver` and `Npgsql` as runtime dependencies because it carried the two provider store implementations, so anyone referencing it resolved both drivers — a consumer keeping compliance records in SQL Server still shipped the MongoDB driver, and did so transitively through `Excalibur.Compliance.Postgres` and `Excalibur.Compliance.SqlServer` as well. The MongoDB and Postgres compliance stores moved into their own packages.
- **Event-id constant classes are now internal.** Fifty-two classes — including `ObservabilityEventId`, `AwsObservabilityEventId`, `DatadogAuditLoggingEventId`, `SentinelAuditLoggingEventId`, `SplunkAuditLoggingEventId`, `DataProcessingEventId` and `DataMySqlEventId` — have been narrowed from public, retiring 189 public constants in that group alone and roughly a tenth of the public surface overall. These are the numeric ids this framework stamps on its own log entries; they were never part of a supported contract, they are not a stable surface, and nothing a consumer writes should depend on them. Because they are `const int`, the compiler inlines them at your compile time, so an application already built against one keeps a working binary and only recompilation is affected. **Filter logs by the numeric event ID, which is unchanged** — that is what the values were always for, and no emitted number moved.
- **Several types are now internal or lose their public constructors, with no behavioural change.** `PostgresOutboxStoreOptionsValidator` and `OracleOutboxStoreOptionsValidator` are internal, matching the SQL Server and in-memory validators; both are registered when you register the store and still run at startup, so a misconfigured failure floor still fails loudly rather than becoming a retry hot-loop. `ElasticsearchRequestLogger` and `ElasticsearchMonitoringService` are internal, having been registered for you by `AddElasticsearchMonitoring()` and resolved through the already-internal client — the monitoring you receive is unchanged, as are the metrics, activities and request logs it emits, and `AddElasticsearchMonitoring()` with its `ElasticsearchMonitoringOptions` is unaffected. `OracleOutboxStore` and `InMemoryOutboxStore` can no longer be constructed directly, matching the SQL Server and Postgres stores; both types stay public, and you register them as you already do. `RedisOutboxStore`'s connection constructor now takes `IConnectionMultiplexer` instead of the concrete `ConnectionMultiplexer` — the builder already accepted the interface, but registration cast it to the concrete type, so supplying a wrapper, proxy or any other implementation failed with an `InvalidCastException` on first resolve.

### Security

- **BREAKING — outbox message-type resolution is bounded by a declared allowlist, and the assembly scan is deleted.** `MessageType` is data read back out of the outbox table, so whatever resolution consults is the set an attacker who can write that table gets to choose from — and the deserializer runs a type's constructors and property setters as it materialises it, so a stored string reached executable code by naming it. Searching loaded assemblies made that set *every type in the process*, and filtering by `IDispatchMessage` narrowed it without bounding it. Resolution now consults only `OutboxDeliveryOptions.MessageTypes`, fixed by the host at composition.
- **BREAKING — Elasticsearch field-encryption integrity validation now actually verifies the authentication tag, and `ValidateIntegrityAsync` takes the field name.** The method answered the question *"has this encrypted field been tampered with?"* by decoding the stored authentication tag and checking it was well-formed Base64. It never used the tag to authenticate anything, so it returned **`true` for any syntactically valid tag — including one an attacker had substituted for the real one.**
- **BREAKING — Elasticsearch field-level encryption refuses to register without a named key provider.** The provider setting silently defaulted to the in-memory development provider, because the enum's zero value is `Local` and an absent configuration key is indistinguishable from a deliberate choice — so a host with field-level encryption enabled encrypted under keys held in a dictionary, and after the next restart that data was unreadable, with no error at the point of loss. A third path was worse: `Hsm` was a defined member with no handling, so explicitly asking for hardware-backed keys was silently downgraded to the same dictionary. **Name the provider explicitly**; registration now throws and names both the configuration key and the deliberate development-mode escape hatch.
- **BREAKING — the security stack composes only the components you name, and message signing is opt-in.** Every `Enable` flag on `SecurityOptions` — `Encryption.EnableEncryption`, `RateLimiting.EnableRateLimiting`, `Authentication.EnableAuthentication` and `Signing.EnableSigning` — now defaults to `false`, and `AddDispatchSecurityMiddleware(IConfiguration)` composes each component only when its `Security:*:Enabled` key is `true`, as it already did for signing, rate limiting and authentication. Signing previously defaulted on, so a host that configured only encryption or rate limiting was given signing as well — and with it a required `IKeyProvider` it had never registered. The framework registers no key provider and never mints signing keys on your behalf: a key nobody chose produces signatures no other instance can verify, and an unverifiable signature is rejected as an authorization failure. So the consequence was not that signing quietly did nothing — resolving `IDispatcher` threw, and **the entire middleware pipeline was lost at host startup**, encryption and rate limiting included.
- **BREAKING — four security switches that enforced nothing are removed.** `SecurityOptions.EnableEncryption`, `EnableSigning`, `EnableRateLimiting` and `EnableValidation` were read by nothing. You could set a security switch, get no error, and get no enforcement — the worst shape a security surface can have, because it reads as protection. They were cut rather than wired, for different reasons per switch: `EnableValidation` and `EnableRateLimiting` each duplicate a live switch that is genuinely read, and `EnableRateLimiting` *contradicts* its live twin by defaulting to `false` where the live one defaults to `true`, so wiring the two together would have silently disabled throttling for every existing consumer; `EnableEncryption` and `EnableSigning` have no mechanism behind them anywhere in the dispatcher, and the only encryption in reach is transport-level and carries its own options. **If you set any of these, you were not getting the behaviour they name — configure the live equivalents instead.**
- **BREAKING — five members of `TransportSecurityOptions` are removed, and none of them influenced your transport's security.** `ValidateServerCertificate`, `MinimumTlsVersion`, `ClientCertificatePath`, `ClientCertificateKeyPath` and `ClientCertificatePassword` are gone, together with the `TlsVersion` enum that existed only to type `MinimumTlsVersion`. No code in the framework read any of them: no certificate was ever loaded from those paths, no password was ever used to open one, and no connection was ever refused for negotiating a TLS version below the configured floor. **`ValidateServerCertificate` defaulted to `true` and `MinimumTlsVersion` to `Tls12`, so both read as protection already in force — which is why they are called out here rather than deleted quietly.** TLS to your broker is unaffected and continues to work exactly as it did: it is established by your endpoint scheme and the platform's own defaults, which these settings never fed into, and `RequireTls` is unchanged and still enforced at connection time. **The consequence worth checking is mutual TLS — if you set `ClientCertificatePath`, you were not presenting a client certificate.** A broker that requires one was either refusing the connection or you were authenticating by another means. Configure client certificates on the transport's own client instead: the Kafka producer configuration's `SslCertificateLocation`, `SslKeyLocation` and `SslKeyPassword`, or the RabbitMQ connection factory's `SslOption`. `TransportSecurityFailureReason.TlsVersionTooLow` and `.ClientCertificateFailed` go with them, having named failures only the deleted TLS-version floor and client-certificate paths could ever have reported; the remaining members keep their existing numeric values, so nothing you have stored or logged changes meaning.
- **BREAKING — the stubbed Elasticsearch threat detectors, the security monitor's start/stop lifecycle, and eight security configuration types are removed.** `IElasticsearchSecurityAnalysis.AnalyzeDataAccessEventAsync` and `PerformThreatDetectionAsync`, with `ThreatDetectionRequest` and `ThreatDetectionResult`, **answered "no threat" to every call**: the unauthorized-access and data-exfiltration checks returned `false` unconditionally, and the brute-force, anomaly and data-threat detectors did nothing at all, so it returned an empty result for every time range. The unconditional-false location-anomaly check inside `AnalyzeAuthenticationEventAsync` is gone for the same reason. If you called either removed method and acted on its result, you were acting on a constant. `IElasticsearchSecurityMonitoring`, `StartMonitoringAsync`, `StopMonitoringAsync`, `IsMonitoring`, `GetMonitoringStatusAsync`, `SecurityMonitoringStatus` and `SecurityMonitoringStatistics` go with them: `StartMonitoringAsync` set a flag and started a timer whose only work was that threat detection — so it started nothing, while `IsMonitoring` reported `true` and `GetMonitoringStatusAsync` returned event, alert and threat counts that were hard-coded zeros rather than measurements. Eight configuration types were bound from `Elasticsearch:Security`, passed validation, and were then read by no code path in the package — `AuthenticationProtectionOptions`, `CertificateAuthenticationOptions`, `SecurityAlertingOptions`, `ThreatIntelligenceOptions`, `FirewallOptions`/`FirewallProvider`/`FirewallAction` and the Elasticsearch `TransportSecurityOptions`, with their parent properties — along with `NetworkSecurityOptions.RequirePrivateNetwork` and `.AllowedNetworkInterfaces`, `SecurityMonitoringOptions.DetectAnomalies`, `.MonitorAuthenticationAttacks` and `.DetectDataExfiltration`, `AuthenticationResult.AccountLocked`/`.CredentialsExpired`/`.MfaRequired`, `SecurityAlertRequest.AutoDistribute` and `SecurityAlertResult.DistributionStatus`. **Several defaulted to `true` — `AuthenticationProtectionOptions.Enabled`, `ValidateCertificateChain`, `CheckCertificateRevocation`, `EnforceEncryption`, `RequirePerfectForwardSecrecy`, `ValidateHostnames`, `DetectAnomalies`, `MonitorAuthenticationAttacks` and `DetectDataExfiltration` — so they read as protection already in force, which is why they are named here rather than deleted quietly.** `SecurityIncidentSeverity` and `SecurityIncidentStatus` were vocabulary for a feature the package does not have — no method took one, no property held one, no incident type existed for them to describe — and are removed too, as are a security policy engine and a security event aggregator that were declared with no members at all and registered as singletons by the security registration, so a container composing a security posture held two objects that enforced and aggregated nothing. **What survives is real and unchanged: `AnalyzeAuthenticationEventAsync` still counts a user's recent failed logins, compares them against `SecurityMonitoringOptions.FailedLoginThreshold`, and raises `ThreatDetected`**; alert generation, severity ranking and storage in Elasticsearch (gated on `StoreAlertsInElasticsearch`) all still work, the registered background service continues to drain and process pending security alerts, and `IElasticsearchSecurityMonitor.Configuration` survives on the composite interface. `ElasticsearchAuthenticationType.Saml` is removed as well: the provider had no arm for it in any of its authentication, validation, refresh or rotation paths and could not select it from configuration, so choosing it produced no credential and then failed as a missing credential rather than as an unsupported method — Elasticsearch SAML sign-on requires an assertion obtained from an identity provider through a browser, which a data-access provider cannot obtain on its own. Authenticate with an API key, a service account or OAuth 2.0, or exchange the SAML assertion yourself and supply the resulting token.
- **The security guide's SOC 2 monitoring and incident-response sections are removed, because the framework does not implement those controls.** `docs/advanced/security.md` documented `AddSecurityMonitoring` taking a configuration lambda with `EnableAnomalyDetection`, `RateLimitThreshold`, `UnusualAccessPatternDetection` and `AddAlertSink<T>()`, and an `AddIncidentResponse` lambda with `AutoEscalationThreshold`, `ResponseTimeTarget`, `AddNotificationChannel<T>()` and an `IIncidentService` resolved from the container. **None of those members exist** — `AddSecurityMonitoring` has no lambda overload at all — so neither example could ever have compiled. Accompanying tables promised that failed logins would "lock account", that data exfiltration would be "blocked", and that unusual access would raise an "MFA challenge"; **the framework performs none of those actions.** What is real is narrower, and worth stating exactly rather than not at all: given authentication and data-access events your own application supplies, the monitor compares a failed-login count against a configured threshold and raises a threat-detected event. **Detecting a threshold breach is not enforcing anything** — responding to the event is your SIEM's or your application's job, and always was. A summary row claiming SOC 2 CC7.2–7.5 coverage has been removed for the same reason.
- **Elasticsearch and OpenSearch request logging redacts body values against an allow list, and can no longer be switched off.** Previously, when body logging was enabled, values were masked by matching four field names — `password`, `apiKey`, `token` and `authorization` — and everything else was written to your log in the clear, so a body carrying `ssn`, `clientSecret`, `privateKey`, `sessionId`, a card number, or any other field nobody had thought to add to that list was recorded verbatim. A `SanitizeSensitiveData` setting also disabled masking altogether.
- **Rotating an AWS KMS key made everything encrypted under the previous key undecryptable.** Rotation disabled the superseded key, under a comment saying it was kept "for decryption", but AWS documents a disabled key as unusable for **every** cryptographic operation, decryption included — so each rotation silently cut off access to all existing ciphertext until an operator manually re-enabled that key. **If you rotate on a schedule, as key-management guidance recommends, you lost the ability to read anything encrypted before the rotation.** The superseded key now stays enabled and is marked instead, so it still decrypts and no longer receives new encryptions, which is what decrypt-only means.
- **Four further AWS KMS defects, and one on HashiCorp Vault.** KMS keys reported version 1 forever and prior versions were unreachable, so rotation was invisible to any audit, `GetKeyVersionAsync` could not return an earlier version, and a version-pinned decrypt had nothing to pin to; each version now carries its own durable alias and records its version on the key itself, so version history survives a restart and is visible to every instance rather than living in one process's memory. `GetActiveKeyAsync` used the purpose as a key identifier, looking for a key literally *named* `encryption` and returning null for any real deployment — silently, because null also means "no active key exists" — and worse, a query with no purpose **provisioned a new customer master key**, a durable, billable, security-relevant side effect from a getter; it now queries by the recorded purpose and returns null when there is nothing to return, so if you relied on it creating a key, call `RotateKeyAsync` explicitly. Key listing filtered purpose by substring of the key *id*, so `payments-encryption` matched a query for `encryption` whatever its purpose was, and a key whose purpose was `encryption` did not match unless its id contained the word; filtering now uses the recorded purpose, so key inventory and compliance reporting are correct in both directions.
